but we have indicated through number that they are this through themselves and not through number, and that they are so many — not by division, but by quantity. ⟦q11.3|13⟧ And again, when we say a stone is two-coloured or five-coloured, and whatever is of continuous quantity, we do not divide the one stone into two or five stones, nor do we cut the colours in it from one another, but we signify that so many are about it and in it, as in a subject, without confusion; no cut or division having come about, or being able to befall the stone, from the continuous quantity of the colours numbered about it; just as no confusion or commixture befalls the colours either from the one subject, the stone, about which they are. ⟦q11.3|14⟧ For the stone, having singleness in its subject, is shown also to possess undividedly the quantity of the colours about it. ⟦q11.3|15⟧ And likewise the colours of the stone itself, as differing from one another in quality, possessing quantity, have the singleness without confusion by concurring again by composition into the one subject of the stone; and the stone is one and the same, neither divided by the quantity of the colours, nor confused by the singleness of the subject. ⟦q11.3|16⟧ And having its existence possessed of different principles, according to one it admits number, according to the other it does not accept it. ⟦q11.3|17⟧ Every number, then, is indicative not of the relation itself of the things — division, I mean, and continuity — but of the quantity of the things of which it is the number, introducing the principle of how much the quantity is, and not of how it exists. ⟦q11.3|18⟧ For how could it introduce with itself the relations of the things, which are both before it and can be known without it, when it has no reflection of kinship toward them, being kept apart from them by their quantity in between? ⟦q11.3|19⟧ For seeing a multitude of men, or of horses, or of oxen, and suchlike, we know that they exist by themselves and that each is specifically divided by hypostases, and that they are this, as I said before, without number; but needing number to indicate their quantity, we in no way make it the cause of the relation, since we have no need of it at all even to know the relation. ⟦q11.3|20⟧ And again, beholding a stone or a flower or an animal of many colours, and things like these, we in no way take up number to know that they are variously coloured; but to wish to know the quantity of the colours in them, we do not deny that we need it.

⟦q11.4|1⟧ [0477] *How number is piously taken up to indicate the difference.*

⟦q11.4|2⟧ So it is not that, in indicating how much the quantity is, number makes or introduces division; rather, it indicates quantity and introduces difference. ⟦q11.4|3⟧ For just as every difference is indicative of some quantity admitting number, introducing the principle of the how-of-being — for what is without quantity is altogether without difference too, as simple in essence and in quality — so too every number of a quantity of some things that differ, being indicative according to the principle of how they are, or of how they subsist, is introductive of the difference of the subjects, and not of the relation. ⟦q11.4|4⟧ And that this is so in the very truth, and that every number is indicative of difference and not of division, the most holy Cyril again bears witness to the account in the letter to Eulogius, writing thus, word for word: « So too in the case of Nestorius: even if he says two natures, signifying the difference of the flesh and of God the Word, yet he no longer confesses the union with us. ⟦q11.4|5⟧ For we, having united these, confess one Christ, one Son, one Lord; and thereafter, one incarnate nature of the Son. » As if he were saying: We, examining the all-wise economy of the mystery, when we wish to show that the difference of the things that came together is preserved after the union, say two natures in this respect and only in this, taking up number in contemplation alone, to indicate the difference; but when we set out precisely the ineffable mode of the union of the mystery, we say one incarnate nature of God the Word. ⟦q11.4|6⟧ For this, as I think, is what he means to show by « But we, having united them, confess one Christ, one Son, one Lord; and thereafter one incarnate nature of the Word. » That is: we, confessing the union, and articulating its mode exactly and with piety, do not use the word that signifies the difference to indicate the union; but, taking the one fittingly of the difference and the other of the union, we keep unconfused the sense of the things signified. ⟦q11.4|7+8⟧ So that it is plain from this that saying two natures was common to him and to Nestorius, so far as knowing the difference goes; but it was no longer common to confess the union too, by saying « one Christ, one Son, one Lord, and one incarnate nature of the Word », which Nestorius would not consent to say. ⟦q11.4|9⟧ For saying, « So too in the case of Nestorius, even if he says two natures, signifying the difference of the flesh and of God the Word, yet he no longer confesses the union with us », means nothing other than this: that Nestorius confesses the difference with us, by saying two natures; but no longer confesses the union with us, not saying « one Christ, one Son, one Lord, and one incarnate nature of the Word ». ⟦q11.4|9⟧ And this is made clear to the lovers of the good, and to those who have been zealous to love the truth, from the teacher's appearing nowhere either forbidding to say two natures after the union — but rather to divide the natures after

⟦q12.1|1⟧ [0480] the union — or teaching that the difference of the things that came together has been abolished after the union, as one can learn from countless of his writings.

⟦q12.1|2⟧ If then the union always stands, and the things united always remain unconfused, and the difference of the things united, on account of which number was taken up according to the Fathers, is always preserved, how is it not necessary — if indeed the union and the things united and their difference always stand and remain and are preserved — both to say two natures piously, to indicate the difference of the things that came together, and again to confess one incarnate nature of God the Word, to show the union according to hypostasis: neither of the expressions (these, that is) being abolished by the other, as it seems to some who make unwisdom a ground of wisdom, lest confusion or division be brought in? ⟦q12.1|3⟧ For if we keep silent about the expression that fittingly indicates the difference, we give confusion room to hold its place; and if, again, we do not say the expression that signifies the union, how are we not ignorant of how to put division to flight? ⟦q12.1|3⟧ But both are always set down and piously said of the same Christ, though not in the same respect, since the things signified are not the same as one another either. ⟦q12.1|4⟧ For difference and union are not the same, even if they both are and are said about the same and of the same. ⟦q12.1|4⟧ For either of these, uttered separately from the other, is both incomplete, so far as indicating the whole mystery goes, not bringing along the other as its advocate, and is shown to carry suspicion: the one of division, because of Nestorius, who denies the union according to hypostasis and does not bear to confess that the uncircumscribed was pleased for our sake to be held by flesh; the other of confusion, because of Apollinarius and Eutyches, who deny the difference of the things that came together after the union, and are ashamed to bring themselves to confess that the invisible fell under our sense because of the natural property of his holy flesh from us.

⟦q12.1|5⟧ So, as has been said, those who wish to be truly pious must both confess one incarnate nature of God the Word, that through this the utmost union may be shown, and again say two natures, that the difference of the things that came together, and are preserved without confusion after the union, may be indicated; and take up number for this and this alone. ⟦q12.1|6⟧ For the Fathers did not, it seems, approve that the difference be indicated by any other expression. ⟦q12.1|7⟧ But if anyone trusts in himself and can devise an expression more fitting for indicating it, [0481] let him not grudge us the benefit; and neither shall we grudge him the instruction — not to say the rashness.

⟦q12.2|1+2⟧ *How one must understand the saying set in the letter of Saint Cyril to Succensus; and that the expression «in two» does not conflict with those who say one incarnate nature of the Word, unless it is taken impiously, in the sense Nestorius held.*

⟦q12.2|3⟧ But if they should say that the proposition of those who say two natures united undividedly after the union conflicts none the less with those who say one incarnate nature of the Word, as Saint Cyril supposes in the second memorandum to Succensus, I set this down as plainly the part of men who either do not know or who maliciously twist the words of the Fathers, and who reckon it more profitable to fight against the truth than to make peace with it. ⟦q12.2|4⟧ For they ought, knowing that we shall render account even for a bare thought to the just Judge of our affairs, reading the Fathers' writings with understanding and with the fear of God, to hasten not to make the words that teach peace and concord a ground of battle and dissension. ⟦q12.2|5⟧ For to whom that looks into it in any way at all is it not evident that the blessed Cyril does not appear to forbid such an expression simply, but as put forward according to the ill sense of Nestorius, who says that the union came about relationally — as the blessed Cyril himself is shown to bear witness to himself at the end of the same letter, making his aim plain to the overly contentious, saying thus: « The addition "undividedly" seems somehow, with us, to be significative of right belief. ⟦q12.2|6⟧ But they do not understand it so. ⟦q12.2|7⟧ For "the undivided" with them is taken in another mode, according to the empty phrases of Nestorius. ⟦q12.2|8⟧ For they say that the man in whom he dwelt is undivided from God the Word by equality of honour, by identity of counsel, by authority. ⟦q12.2|9⟧ So that they do not put forward the words simply, but with a certain guile and malice. » Behold, this wonderful teacher clearly supposed the mode of "the undivided" to be twofold: the one signifying the union according to hypostasis, which he also embraces, and says is significative of right belief; the other indicative of a conjunction by relation alone, which he also turns away from, and exhorts the faithful to do this very thing, since from the likeness of utterance in the word it can often slip the poison of heretical impiety unnoticed into the more guileless. ⟦q12.2|10⟧ Whence, with himself, he sets down saying two undividedly as orthodox, since he himself said the union was according to hypostasis; but as [0484] put forward by the followers of Nestorius, he makes the word "undivided" suspect, since they held that the union came about according to the relational conjunction alone.

⟦q12.2|11⟧ Union according to hypostasis, then, according to the Fathers, is the concourse of things of other essence into one hypostasis, holding the natural property of each of the things composed in it, relative to the other, unadulterated and unaltered and undivided. ⟦q12.2|12⟧ Relational union is the equal movement of will and the invariance of the identity of inclination of those who subsist by themselves, specifically, in personal monads. ⟦q12.2|13⟧ He then who confesses the union according to hypostasis, even if he says two natures in Christ after the union, united undividedly — by the union, that is, according to hypostasis — in no way whatever misses the truth. ⟦q12.2|14⟧ For he confesses that the things of which Christ is composed have remained unconfused, because of the difference preserved according to the Fathers, being able in no way to know the things that differ from one another by natural principle as outside the one according to hypostasis, and apart, in any mode whatever. ⟦q12.2|15⟧ But he who champions the union by relation, even if he says "the undivided" ten thousand times, carries the blame: for he says the things signified are united, and have undividedness, by a certain loving co-disposition, being in no way able to say that God the Word, with his own animate and intelligent flesh from us, is one hypostasis. ⟦q12.2|16+17+18⟧ For that one hypostasis should come to be from two specific hypostases, partitioned by a specific principle from the individuals of the same species, is impossible. ⟦q12.2|16+17+18⟧ So our holy Father Cyril does not simply slander and dismiss saying two natures united undividedly after the union, but the sense signified by such an expression in those who use it badly.

⟦q12.2|16+17+18⟧ If then it has been shown — even if moderately, yet, to speak for myself, sufficiently, as far as was possible to signify by letter, both from the holy Fathers and from the sequence of common notions — that number can neither make nor suffer nor introduce division of any kind, but simply indicates how many things are, and does not touch their relation, however they may happen to stand: what suspicion of cut or division remains for those who rebel against the holy Church of God and cut themselves off at random from the common body — not doing more than suffering, being pitiably deadened and corrupted by the separation, and preparing for themselves the penalty, so far as concerns the indignity to the body of Christ — so that on this account, at least, they should refuse the right and pious confession of the faith? ⟦q12.2|16+17+18⟧ Unless indeed, under a pretext of reverence, lest they seem to cut the union, embracing [0485] confusion, they contrive like knaves not to be caught in impious belief.

⟦q12.2|16+17+18⟧ *That in Christ the difference is of things, that is, of essences, and not of qualities; and that every difference of necessity introduces with itself the quantity, altogether, of the things it is said to be of; and the quantity, the number that signifies it, whether the quantity is continuous or divided.*

⟦q12.3|1⟧ For they ought — if this were truly not so — not to refuse to say what and how many the different things are of which they confess the difference to be: they who put forward the difference not even as of things, but by bare and sole utterance, as though it were predicated of nothing, ironically, to deceive the simpler. ⟦q12.3|2+3+4+5+6⟧ For if they believe that the things from which the union is are preserved after the union, they would altogether confess two things preserved. ⟦q12.3|2+3+4+5+6⟧ For so many too are the things that came together into the indissoluble union, having received from the union no change whatever, or confusion, or diminution, or coalescence, or alteration into one another. ⟦q12.3|2+3+4+5+6⟧ But as it is, not holding this, as men who have denied that the natures are preserved after the union, they have made themselves liable to refutation, even if they do not wish it, saying that the difference is preserved in quality alone, apart from the things — which is plainly the part of men at play, and of men who, as is likely, count it an insult not to be laughed at, and who clearly empty the Gospel, legislating that qualities can be without underlying essences, and supposing that difference can be recognized without quantity. ⟦q12.3|2+3+4+5+6⟧ For the most wise, it seems, did not know that every quality is altogether of an underlying essence, about which it is both contemplated and said, having in no way being by itself; and that every difference, being indicative of the unlikeness and otherness of some things toward others according to the principle of how they are, is by nature such as to introduce with itself the quantity of the things that differ, whether essences, or qualities, or properties. ⟦q12.3|7⟧ And simply, to speak in sum, of anything else whatever, of all the things they would suppose to differ from one another and say there is a difference, they have recognized the difference, by sense or by intellection, by contemplating the things that differ altogether in quantity. ⟦q12.3|8+9⟧ For to affirm that one can show difference without quantity is inadmissible: since things that are not in every way the same as one another will in no way admit one and the same principle of being. ⟦q12.3|10⟧ For of essence, or nature, say, toward essence, or of quality toward quality, or of property toward property, or of anything else whatever of the things that are not in every way the same — for things in every way the same as one another do not admit difference either — we can never say one and the same principle of the how-of-being. ⟦q12.3|11⟧ And that one and the same principle cannot be contemplated in all in the same respect — which is what made the difference — reasonably establishes that those things cannot be one and the same as one another whose principle of being is different and not the same. ⟦q12.3|12⟧ And in saying that one thing differs from another, or some things from others, we signify many, or at the least two, and not one; even if in another way, and according to another mode and principle, we say they are of one, and can be one: [0488] as the qualities and properties about it are of one fire, and the natures from which and in which he is known to be constituted are of one Christ, and the things from which he was composed, and in which he is known to exist, are of one man. ⟦q12.3|13⟧ And one in hypostasis, in the case both of Christ and of the man as we are — that is, in the whole made of these as of parts, as receiving properly, both, the different principles of being of the parts in their fullness; but not one in nature and essence, since the parts do not receive the principle of being of one another, nor can we know the same principle of being in both. ⟦q12.3|14⟧ For the principle of Godhead and of manhood is not the same, just as neither is that of soul and body, as is plain to all. ⟦q12.3|15⟧ Wherefore the principle of truth has never defined Christ to be one in nature, nor simply one, or composite, nature. ⟦q12.3|15⟧ For the name Christ is not indicative of essence or nature, as of a species predicated of many individuals differing in their hypostases; as the blessed Cyril says in the Scholia: « That the name Christ neither has the force of a definition, nor indeed shows the essence of anything; but of the hypostasis of the Word, understood, that is, in the assumption of flesh having an intellectual soul. » Nor has it defined man as one nature, the one from soul and body, as though the body were of the same essence as the soul; but as one in species: cut off from the other species by the constitutive difference, and predicated equally of the individuals under it as a species and contained by it. ⟦q12.3|16⟧ And it has not, on that account, forbidden that soul and body, as soul and body, be two according to the essential principle, and other and other essence according to the principle of being — the things from which, and in which, and which man is.

⟦q12.4|1⟧ *That it is impious, and in conflict with the truth, to say that Christ is one composite nature.*

⟦q12.4|2+3⟧ For every composite nature, having its own parts contemporaneous with itself and with one another in its very coming-to-be into being, and brought forth from not-being into being, and to the completion of the ordering of the universe, by the power that constituted the universe and holds it together in being, has its own parts, of necessity and fittingly, contained by one another: as is the case with man, and with the other things that have had the lot of being of composite nature — the soul both holding the body involuntarily and being held by it, and supplying life to it without choice, by the mere fact of its being in it, and partaking naturally of passion and hurt because of the power receptive of these that lies in it — even if the

⟦q13.1|1⟧ [0489] …especially certain of those who, having strayed from the true doctrines of the Church concerning the soul — telling the marvel that the soul pre-exists its own body, in the Greek manner, or exists after it, in the Jewish manner, and supposing a beginning neither good nor God-pleasing to precede the fashioning of visible things — are disposed in antipathy toward such an account; whose unsound and discordant problems on this head I have judged it not to belong to the present occasion and discourse to refute, and I shall return to what lies before us.

⟦q13.1|2⟧ Since, then, as has been set out, every composite nature is so disposed, none of those who have learned to think piously about divine things would ever dare to say that Christ is one composite nature; lest, choosing to say such things, by a certain natural train and consequence of necessity — supposing him wholly created and from things that are not, circumscribed and passible, and not consubstantial with the Father; and either the flesh coeternal with the Word, or the Word, being of one time with the flesh, for such is the principle of every composite nature — he should make himself liable to fearful charges. ⟦q13.1|3⟧ For what is of a composite nature is also, clearly, composite according to nature. ⟦q13.1|4⟧ And what is composite according to nature could never be connatural and consubstantial with what is simple according to nature. ⟦q13.1|5⟧ It is not right, then, for the pious to say that Christ is one composite nature, not only because of the absurdity that is gathered from such an expression, but because none of the God-chosen Fathers has ever been shown to have said this; but rather to confess one composite hypostasis of Christ, and two natures; so that he may be known as consubstantial and connatural with the Father according to the Godhead, and the same be recognized as consubstantial and connatural with us according to the flesh; and that one and the same, toward both the Father and us, be believed to be mediator of God and men.

⟦q13.2|1⟧ *On composite hypostasis.*

⟦q13.2|2+3⟧ But if those who speak against us should say that we too — since every composite hypostasis is shown to have its own parts of one time both with itself and with one another, and we do not deny saying that Christ is a composite hypostasis — cannot shake off the reproach on this point, as caught by our own words: we say it would truly be so, if we supposed it to be composite as having a species predicated of it. ⟦q13.2|4⟧ But as it is, not saying this, we incur no reproach of any kind whatever, knowing clearly that every composite hypostasis that falls under some species is composite not on its own account, but on account of the nature that contains it, when that nature is composite, and of the predicated species under which it is naturally brought; since the particular, that is the specific, holds in itself the universal — that is, the common and generic — whole and most fully, while the proper imparts nothing at all of its own principle to the generic: which, in the case of Christ, no one at all of those who from of old down to us [0492] have been expounders of the sense of the divine sayings has ever proclaimed; in whose case no one will be able to find either genus or species as predicated of Christ. ⟦q13.2|5⟧ For not by the principle of nature did the thearchic Word sojourn among us through flesh; but by a mode of economy, having united our nature to himself according to hypostasis without deficiency, he renewed it. ⟦q13.2|6+7⟧ So they have profited nothing from their great cleverness — to take up the sequence of what was examined a little before — since the clever device has turned back upon them again into the quantity they fled, so that they cannot say that even the difference in quality itself is without quantity. ⟦q13.2|8⟧ For it is altogether necessary that the qualities whose difference they suppose there to be be many, or at the least two, as has been said before. ⟦q13.2|9⟧ For that a single quality, holding itself altogether unmixed with respect to another, differs from itself, no one who has a mind would ever say.

⟦q13.2|10⟧ It remains, then, either that they desist even from bare difference itself, having learned that it is wholly indicative of a quantity of certain things that differ; or that they accept along with us the confession of the truth — with us who, by the principle of the difference and of it only, for the knowledge that the things which came together have remained unconfused, piously take up the number in accordance with the Fathers. ⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ Otherwise, let them tell us, who wish lovingly to ask — if there is in them a word of wisdom not ignoble — how, saying that the things which ran together into union are two, and not denying that these same have remained unconfused even after the union, as neither has departed from its natural definition and principle, even if both together make up one person of the Son and one hypostasis, they do not confess as two the things that are preserved even by their own account, as they say, if they speak the truth? ⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ And what is their account of the diminution, and for what cause, and how have both the natures become one? ⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ Or, if the union has remained pure of diminution, what is the account of the things preserved without diminution not being spoken of? ⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ Then we readily acquiesce in the privation of the one, and admire the vigour of their erudition, as of those alone shown to us capable of the knowledge of things unspeakable. ⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ But they will never be able to produce the argument that persuades of this.

⟦q13.2|11+12+13⟧ *A concise solution of Severus's difficulty against the expression "In two".*

⟦q13.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Even if, above all, they flee at last to that unsound and empty invention of Severus — the one he carries about everywhere and sings as something clever — saying that it is ridiculous to say that from two natures two natures are again displayed from the union, drawing the likely conclusions as from a premise granted by us: from whom of those instructed in the divine doctrines of the Church did they hear that the union from two natures produced two natures? ⟦q13.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ But they will by no means be able to say that they have heard it, if they care for truth. ⟦q13.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ We say that the union came to be from two natures; but that the one [0493] hypostasis of Christ brought to completion from them is composite, keeping and preserving those very natures from which it was composed — as a whole its own parts — without deficiency, with their natural idioms, and without change; and kept and preserved in them, as a whole in parts. ⟦q13.3|6⟧ From two natures, then, we say that Christ is not two natures, but one composite hypostasis; of these as of its own parts; comprehensive of the natures, and containing these as its own and completing parts; and in them, as in parts, both being and being recognized. ⟦q13.3|7⟧ For as long as the things which came together are believed to have remained unconfused — and they remain unconfused always — it is impossible for them not to be two in respect of their naturally being what each naturally is according to essence; just as, then, for these not to be one by the principle of the hypostasis, as long as the union exists — and it exists always, according to the pious confession of the faithful — is also impossible.

⟦q13.3|8⟧ Therefore, saying that the same is one and two, we do not say both of the same and one thing in the same respect, but in one respect and in another. ⟦q13.3|9+10⟧ For instance, according to the principle of the nature of the things from which the union is, the "two": for we do not know God the Word to be the same as his own flesh according to nature; but according to the principle of the hypostasis, the "one": for we know God the Word to be the same as his own flesh according to hypostasis. ⟦q13.3|11⟧ Neither, then, do we senselessly fuse the natures into one nature, by saying that the natural difference in Christ has been abolished, lest we introduce a change of the Word and of the flesh; nor again do we madly divide them into two self-subsistent beings, by positing a difference also according to the principle of the hypostasis, lest we deny our own salvation: as did those who, from diametrically opposite sides, parted the impiety between them, Apollinarius together with Eutyches, and Nestorius — the former, not knowing a natural difference in Christ; the latter, adding to it a personal one as well — and fell from the truth. ⟦q13.3|12⟧ But knowing that even in the union according to hypostasis the natural property is unchangeable, we turn away from confusion and from division alike, making neither the union confusion, as though ignorant of the things united, nor the difference division, as though knowing the things that differ to subsist by themselves in partition.

⟦q13.3|13⟧ This, then, as I think, the great Theologian Gregory too appears to teach in the great Apologetic, saying «One from both, and both through one»; as though he said: For just as one from both — that is, from two natures the one, as a whole from parts, according to the principle of the hypostasis — so also through the one according to hypostasis, as through a whole, both, by the principle of nature, the parts: that is, the two. ⟦q13.3|14⟧ Which, going through it more clearly in the second oration on the Son, he says: «For even if the combination is one, yet not [0496] in nature, but in concourse.» ⟦q13.3|14⟧ And this very thing the blessed Cyril too is shown to think in his letters to Succensus, in which, on the example of man, he says: «So that the two are no longer two, but through both the one living being is brought to completion»; all but crying out explicitly in these words that, for the things from which the composition is — both in the man that we are, who is from soul and body, and in the mystery of Christ, which is from Godhead and manhood — their being two according to the essential principle has not been abolished; since otherwise not even otherness of essence would still remain. ⟦q13.3|15⟧ But that they are two by themselves, in monad and monad, self-subsistently, the principle of piety does not admit at all; lest we divide the one man, or living being, into two men or living beings, and the one Christ into two Christs, or two Sons. ⟦q13.3|16⟧ For if the blessed Cyril had not known well to think and to hold and to teach this everywhere, he would not have said that the difference of the natures was not abolished through the union; he would not have taught that the one Christ is one thing and another, and other and other, and this and that, and both, and that the natures have remained unconfused. ⟦q13.3|17⟧ He would not have held that the Word did not pass over into the nature of the flesh, or that the flesh was not converted into the nature of the Word himself. ⟦q13.3|18⟧ He would not have known that the God-speaking men make the evangelical and apostolic sayings about the Lord some common, as of one person, and others divided, as of two natures. ⟦q13.3|19⟧ He would not have declined to say that the whole, that is Christ, is one nature, if he had known this to be pious. ⟦q13.3|20⟧ He would not have defined human nature as nothing other than flesh intellectually ensouled, affirming through this that the nature of manhood too is perfect in Christ. ⟦q13.3|21⟧ He would not have said that by adding "Incarnate" the signification of our essence is brought in. ⟦q13.3|22⟧ He would not have determined that to say two natures united without division is significant of right belief, when the expression is not mischievously put forward in the mode Nestorius held. ⟦q13.3|23+24⟧ He would not have been seen saying such things — no, countless others — as is clear to the diligent from his writings.

⟦q13.3|25⟧ Whence, then, and from which of the teachers, will they demonstrate that the natures in Christ, from which he also is — preserved after the union, as they say, even by their own account — ought not to be piously confessed by the faithful as two natures? ⟦q13.3|26⟧ For no one at all of the approved teachers is found to have decreed this; on the contrary, all both said it, and taught the faithful through their own words to say it; unless indeed, fashioning from their heart, as is their custom, what seems good to them, they should put forward the fiction as from one of the Fathers, for the deception of the more simple. ⟦q13.3|27⟧ But these men, as it seems, deeming it a shame to be persuaded, and preferring their attachment to the men who deceived them from the beginning [0497] to the divine love, and honouring contentiousness above piety, make the acceptance and honour from God for the concord of the faith lower than being honoured as priests by men — to whom they bring the deception by lying against us of things that are not — and than receiving evil in return for evils, I mean the perishable things as the wage of deception; and they do not cease from warring against the truth and attempting to draw away from it as many as they can.

⟦q13.3|28⟧ Therefore I, the least, exhort you — being genuine children of the holy apostolic Church, in which, illumined through the regeneration of the divine washing, you were deemed worthy to be adopted to God in the Spirit — to withdraw from such men, remembering the holy Apostle Paul writing to the Romans in this manner: I exhort you, brethren, to watch those who make the dissensions and the stumbling-blocks contrary to the teaching which you learned, and turn away from them. ⟦q13.3|29+30⟧ For such men do not serve our Lord Christ, but their own belly; and through fair speech and blessing they deceive the hearts of the innocent. [n: Rom. xvi, 17, 18.] And again, concerning these same men, accustomed to stir up troubles in the Church of God, writing to the Corinthians thus: For such are false apostles, deceitful workers, transforming themselves into Apostles of Christ. ⟦q13.3|31⟧ And no marvel; for Satan himself is transformed into an angel of light. ⟦q13.3|32+33⟧ It is no great thing, then, if his ministers also are transformed as ministers of righteousness; whose end shall be according to their works. [n: II Cor. xi, 13-15.] And the deceitfulness of the inclination of these very men, and the stealth of their manner, the Lord in the Gospels, hinting at it, proclaimed beforehand, as it were securing the faithful, saying: Beware of the false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravening wolves. ⟦q13.3|34+35⟧ By their fruits you shall know them. [n: Matth. vii, 15, 16.] And concerning these very men the holy Evangelist John too, in his first catholic epistle, making the pious more sober, says: Beloved, believe not every spirit, but test the spirits, whether they are of God; for many false prophets have gone out into the world. ⟦q13.3|36+37⟧ Hereby know the Spirit of God: every spirit that confesses Jesus Christ come in the flesh is of God. ⟦q13.3|38⟧ And every spirit that does not confess the Lord Jesus Christ to have come in the flesh is not of God; and this is ⟨that of the Antichrist, of which you have heard that it comes, and⟩ even now is in the world. [n: I Joan. iv, 1-3.]

⟦q13.3|39⟧ Remembering these words always, I ask you, blessed ones — I wish you all to hold fast, with all attention and care, to piety, than which nothing is more proper to men for being made friends with God; turning away with all strength from those who do not accept the pious and saving doctrines of the Church; and above all, having learned that it is of the teaching of the Antichrist not to confess in the flesh our Lord

⟦q14.1|1+2⟧ [0500] and God Jesus Christ, as the great evangelist John explicitly cries out; [n: I Joan. iv, 3.] which those who now war against the Church of God openly proclaim, denying the nature of the flesh by not enduring to confess that it is preserved and exists in Christ; and thus they do not shudder — the more pitiable thing, or, to say it more properly, the bolder — at putting themselves forward as heralds of the opinion opposed to God. ⟦q14.1|1+2⟧ But these men, having chosen to be so dangerously disposed in respect of the faith, reap [?] a fruit worthy of their own folly: to have acquired a refutation of their impiety that is easily detected by all.

⟦q14.2|1⟧ *A concise exposition of the acceptance of the expressions of the faith that are rightly confessed concerning Christ according to the catholic Church.*

⟦q14.2|2⟧ But we — with God be it said — who feed on the divine and pure pasture of the ecclesiastical teaching, having the glory of piety unassailable, make bold to confess before God and men, piously, all the pious expressions of the Fathers concerning Christ, receiving each with pious intention and fitting reasoning; just as from above and from the beginning, through those who became ministers of grace, we were catechized in the saving word of the faith. ⟦q14.2|2⟧ We confess, then, our Lord Jesus Christ from two natures, as composed of Godhead and manhood; and in two natures, knowing him as being in Godhead and manhood. ⟦q14.2|3⟧ For just as, saying "from two natures", we hold that he is from Godhead and manhood, so also, saying "in two natures", we fittingly signify that he exists in Godhead and manhood; as being separated, after the union, from none of the natures of which he is constituted, but both being and being known in them, from which he also always is. ⟦q14.2|4⟧ And again we confess him two natures united according to hypostasis without division, believing the same to be truly God and man, and that he verifies the names by the very realities. ⟦q14.2|5⟧ For as we believe him both to be and to be called Son of God and God by nature and in truth, because of the identity of essence with the God and Father, so also we say that he both is and is called Son of man, and man become, by nature and in truth, because of the natural kinship according to the flesh with her who bore him. ⟦q14.2|6⟧ For if we do not confess him two natures, as at once God and man, we shall be convicted of saying that he is composed of bare names and not of realities, and is not in truth that which is signified through the names to be. ⟦q14.2|7⟧ So that — by those very men's account — he who chooses to blaspheme could at last suppose, so to say, that what is worshipped and adored by us is nothing: which has passed beyond every excess of impiety.

⟦q14.2|8⟧ Wherefore, guarding ourselves [0501] against being brought under a charge of blasphemy, we do not cease confessing Christ from two natures, as from Godhead, as has been said, and manhood; and in two natures, as in Godhead and manhood; and two natures, as believing the same to be at once God and man; since we know him to be not only from these, but also these; nor these only, but also in these; as a whole from parts, and a whole in parts; and being a whole through the parts. ⟦q14.2|9⟧ For if he who gave being to the things that are also granted them the power for permanence, so that the species should not be commixed with one another nor be converted into one another, but each remain according to the principle by which it has come to be from him — so that, from the stability of the natural property of each, those who love contemplation and are diligent about divine things may conceive, as far as is attainable, the all-wise and knowing Maker and Marshal of the universe — much more would he confirm in himself his own infinitely powerful power: remaining what he was, and becoming what he was not; and not denying what he became, together with remaining what he was, by being both without deficiency and without change.

⟦q14.2|10+11+12⟧ And we say piously also one nature of God the Word incarnate — incarnate in flesh having the intellectual and rational soul — bringing in, by adding "Incarnate", the signification of our essence. ⟦q14.2|10+11+12⟧ For it is a periphrasis that shows in one and the same, by way of union, the two natures, through a name and a definition: through a name, "the one nature of the Word", which signifies the common of the essence together with the proper of the hypostasis; through a definition, the human nature, as the teacher himself says: «For what is the nature of manhood, except that it is flesh intellectually ensouled?» ⟦q14.2|10+11+12⟧ So that he who says "one nature of God the Word incarnate" shows that God the Word is with ensouled flesh, understanding the flesh to be something wholly other according to essence than God the Word — he, at least, who is the true and unadulterated disciple of piety. ⟦q14.2|13⟧ And saying "the union according to hypostasis", we both understand and confess that the union of the natures has come to be into one hypostasis; so that neither is or is understood at all by itself, but with the one compounded and grown together with it; nor again is it commixed with the other according to the essential principle, or has it suffered from the union, in any respect whatever, diminution of its natural perfection. ⟦q14.2|14⟧ And again we confess "the natural union" in the sense of saying "true", just as the holy Cyril himself both said and explained these expressions; and not for the destruction, after the union, of the two natures from which Christ is, or of one of them, as Apollinarius, Eutyches and Severus, following Simon Magus, Valentinus and Manes, have set it forth; as it is easy for those who wish to see, from the writings of Severus himself, through the agreement of the words, the [0504] identity of his doctrines with those of the impious men named.

⟦q14.2|15⟧ Likewise we say that the two births are of the same one Christ: the one from God the Father before the ages, and the one that came to be for us in the last times from the holy Virgin; and likewise we glorify as of the same both the miracles and the passions. ⟦q14.2|16⟧ We confess also the holy all-glorious Virgin properly and in truth Theotokos, as having become mother not of some mere man — even if formed beforehand, as in the twinkling of an eye, apart from the union with the Word, and deified by progress of works and by the summit of virtue — but truly of God the Word himself, the one of the holy Trinity, incarnate from her by an ineffable conception, and perfectly become man.

⟦q14.2|16⟧ These expressions, then, the holy Church of God, having taught her own piously both to say and to understand, has entrusted to be guarded by the divine and bright light of knowledge; exhorting, through both Prophets and Apostles, and through Christ himself, who by his own sufferings fastened her together and fitted her, to take up a life suited and fitting to the great profession of faith: Proving what is the will of God, the good and acceptable and perfect; and keeping watch unto it. ⟦q14.2|17⟧ For we have been taught that we need the best conduct no less than pious faith for attaining everlasting life; lest, building on the foundation of the faith things that do not belong to it, by wicked practices, as having outraged the calling and the grace of the adoption, we make ourselves liable, instead of joy and endless glory, to everlasting fire.

⟦q14.2|18⟧ Seeing, then, many ways of virtues, you blessed ones and sober lovers of the blessed beauty of Christ — ways given to men by the Lord of all, having one goal, and all arriving at one end, unto salvation — let us not neglect our own salvation, nor let slip the time given us for repentance, which it is impossible to attain again. ⟦q14.2|19⟧ But before we go, as the great Job says somewhere, whence we shall not return, to a land dark and gloomy, to a land of everlasting darkness, where there is no light, nor to see the life of mortals, let us be eager to propitiate God, each according to his own power. ⟦q14.2|20⟧ For in view of the variety and difference of the states of our souls, he, as good, has fittingly made many also the ways that lead those who travel by them blamelessly to the everlasting mansions; so that each, choosing the one suited to himself, and running the course of his life by it, may attain the all-glorious place laid up in the heavens according to our hopes.

⟦q14.2|21⟧ Therefore, as we have power, by the co-operation and grace of Christ, let us lay hold of our duties. ⟦q14.2|22⟧ Let us lighten with compassion the misfortune of the poor man, and let us not [0505] neglect to make his want more moderate. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ Let us not put off covering the nakedness of the naked, revering the garment of incorruption given to us. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ Let us suffer with those worn down in the affliction of prison and in weakness of body, and pressed down by the burden of exile, and let us be eager to make the hardships of others our own; so that, imitating, toward our own kind, him who Took our infirmities and bore our diseases, [n: Matth. viii, 17; Isa. liii, 4.] and honouring by our works his self-emptying, in love for man, for our sake, we too may be able to become worthy beholders and sharers of his glory; not practising these things for the sake of the glory from men, of which, after the present life, nothing subsists toward the reckoning of acceptance; but becoming wholly God's in truth, and serving him, as one who both is and becomes all things to all, [n: I Cor. ix, 22.] through love for man toward one another, let us both say and do all things by which we shall please him. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ For thus, by progress in virtue little by little, we shall be able — with God be it said — at some time also fittingly to pursue the divine philosophy, and to be transferred to the life above, having despised all things on earth; considering it better and more honourable to hold the last rank with God than to bear the first things of those on earth with the king below: in which, beginning to converse with ourselves, and having accustomed the senses of the soul, quietly, in stillness, to see creation wisely, we shall carry off the greatest gain — to be well guided through the turmoil and instability of visible things to the things that stand and are not moved; and not to remain among them, nor to endure being held fast in them; from which, hating the disorder of the one and longing for the firm footing of the other, we shall call on abyss with abyss; [n: Psal. xli, 8.] perceiving, through the mighty works in visible things, the principles in intelligible things, as far as is attainable to men; and thus, simply, through speechlessness, peering into the inmost sanctuary of the sanctuaries, we shall come to know our own worthlessness, being mystically taught through the oracles of the Spirit how, being earth and ashes, [n: Gen. xviii, 27.] we lay claim to divine glory, by the grace of him who was well pleased; and into what kind of blessedness what is by nature worthless and contemptible is refashioned. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ And thus, struck with amazement at the spectacles of the infinite, we shall gather humility, the strong safeguard of the virtues, God's greatest and first gift, which is the fruit of the true philosophy; through which, mortifying the causes of the passions — I mean the senses — together with the kindred affinity of sensible things toward them, we cut ourselves off from the world altogether, so as to be able to apply ourselves to intelligible things with an undisturbed eye of the soul. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ Out of which, or after which, the divine love, taking over the soul, now truly qualified by humility and henceforth become capable of receiving the image of the divine reflection, makes us friends instead of servants, bestowing the summary of the law, the: You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with [0508] all your soul, and so on, [n: Matth. xxii, 37.] in permitting none of the intellectual powers in us to reach out at all to anything else than, manfully, to God.

⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ Let us strive, then, while we have the time set before us by God, [n: Galat. vi, 10.] to bring to work the goal of the pious and Christian profession. ⟦q14.2|23+24+25+26+27+28+29⟧ And since Our wrestling is not against blood and flesh, but against the principalities, against the powers, against the world-rulers of the darkness of this age — the unseen tyrants and enemies, who do not join battle in the open, but entwine their wars in the hidden place of the heart — Let us take up the whole armour of God, as the divine Apostle exhorts us, that we may be able to withstand in the evil day, and having accomplished all, to stand; and having girded our loins in truth, and having put on the breastplate of righteousness, in which all the fiery darts of the evil one shall be quenched. [n: Ephes. vi, 12.] Let us persevere in all prayer and supplication, and receive the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God, which divides — to put it briefly — the worse from the better, and teaches the pious the conduct fitting for Christians, which is the work of perfect discernment: exactness of the eyes, continence of the tongue, discipline of the body, a lowly mind, purity of thought, the abolition of anger, as the great Basil says somewhere; the mind's flight up to God; and to struggle against temptations for the guarding of precious things; to add more when pressed into service; not to go to law when robbed; to measure out love in return for hatred; to match endurance to persecutions; exhortation to blasphemies; to die to sin; to be crucified with Christ; to transfer love wholly to God, without relation.

⟦q14.2|30⟧ These things I have written, my blessed master, not being ignorant of your firmness of faith in Christ, and the dignity growing in him through the commandments; but wishing you to know of what kind and how great a struggle I have for you, ever burning up my heart with care; and I exhort you to watch the champions of the heresies more soberly, lest any of them, having beguiled you with the persuasive speech of falsehood, should be able to muddy the clear and living stream of the faith in you with the filth proper to the impious doctrines — which may it not be. ⟦q14.2|31⟧ For they possess a tongue that is a two-edged sword and a sharpened razor, slaughtering souls, and sending them on to the trap of Hades and the pit of darkness, and depriving them of the life in Christ through their deluded fair speech. ⟦q14.2|32⟧ But [0509] our Lord and God Jesus Christ himself, the only-begotten Son and Word of God and the Father, the Maker of all creation visible and invisible, who for his unspeakable love for man, as he himself was well pleased, accepted death for us, and rescued us by his own blood from the power of darkness: may he himself hold your right hand, and guide you into his fear, and fill you with the knowledge of his will in all wisdom and spiritual understanding; and persuade you to walk worthily of him, bearing fruit in every good work, and growing in the knowledge of his truth; and strengthen you with all power, according to the might of his glory, unto all edification and long-suffering; and, keeping you striving together, with joy and thanksgiving, for the gospel of his grace, deem you worthy of the portion of the saints in light, [n: ibid. 9-12.] by the prayers and supplications of our all-glorious Lady, the Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary, and of all the saints. ⟦q14.2|33⟧ Amen.

⟦q15.1|1⟧ LETTER 13

⟦q15.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to Peter the illustris: a concise discourse against the doctrines of Severus.*

⟦q15.1|2⟧ My blessed master has truly shown that bodily absence from one another can do nothing toward the diminution of spiritual love, for those who are intellectually fitted to one another through love according to God; not having disdained to visit, through his precious syllables, me, the lowly and sinner, who has nothing worthy of his love. ⟦q15.1|3⟧ Which having received, I embraced and greeted them rejoicing, as if he himself were present in the spirit, and gave thanks, as is my custom, to the only God and Saviour of all, Christ, having learned that he had accomplished the watery way in safety. ⟦q15.1|3⟧ For truly sweet is my blessed master when present, and when absent, no less than when present, he is lovable to all, because of the grace of the poverty of the Holy Spirit [n: Matth. v, 3.] that shines upon him; through which everyone who genuinely lays claim to this, as the virtue of God, becomes a holy temple for a dwelling of God in the Spirit — of him who humbled himself unto the form of a servant, and by work and word showed it in himself to men: whom imitating, my blessed master too has inwardly acquired both meekness and humility. ⟦q15.1|4+5+6+7⟧ Meekness, that, quenching the passionate movements of anger and desire, he may become well-pleasing to God and men. ⟦q15.1|4+5+6+7⟧ For meekness is nothing other than [0512] a complete immobility of anger and desire toward what is contrary to nature; by which the divine will becomes manifest to those who have it. ⟦q15.1|4+5+6+7⟧ For the great David, singing psalms, says: He will guide the meek in judgement; he will teach the meek his ways. [n: Psal. xxiv, 9.] And humility, that, cutting out the callosities of pride, he may become both grateful to God and approachable to all men. ⟦q15.1|8⟧ For humility is each one's recognition, by juxtaposition with God side by side, of the transcendent and productive cause; by which the co-disposition of men toward one another naturally comes to be, and the remission of offences is given to those who owe. ⟦q15.1|9⟧ For see, says the same great David again, my humility and my toil, and forgive all my sins. [n: ibid. 18.]

⟦q15.1|10⟧ Being thus, then, in regard to life, my blessed master I pray may be preserved in the firmness of faith in Christ, unshaken as his name declares, and above every heretical billow, as a genuine child of the holy catholic and apostolic Church of God; founded upon the pious confession, against which the wicked mouths of the heretics, opened like the gates of Hades, have no strength at all. ⟦q15.1|11⟧ For concerning the bastards, and those bastardly grafted in from the wild olive into the good olive, I knew, even before you blessed ones wrote, that, according to the prophetic word, As a dog to its own vomit, [n: Prov. xxvi, 11.] they are bound to return — I mean to the stench of the loathsomeness of their faith; as second Simons, and Demases and Hermogeneses, having chosen to prefer falsehood to truth; and, as they think, wronging the heralds of piety in no way by their apostasy, but rather being justly harmed themselves by the deprivation of grace; and not even able to have to their heart's content the present age, for whose sake they departed from the truth, since it is by nature unstable and easily altered, and ever runs past those who think they hold it.

⟦q15.1|11⟧ For truly loathsome and full of stench, and empty of all grace, is every account that does not maintain that the things from which Christ is are preserved according to nature in the one Christ after the ineffable union; but as it were denies the whole economy of God concerning man, and cuts off from the Word of God what was assumed from the immaculate and holy all-glorious Virgin and in truth Theotokos: a rational, ensouled body. ⟦q15.1|12⟧ For truly he cuts it off in fact, even if he seems somehow barely to confess it with the mouth, for the deception of the hearers, and he denies the incarnation altogether, who does not say that after the union the nature of the flesh is, is preserved and is spoken of, united without confusion and without division to God the Word according to hypostasis — on the pretext, forsooth, of reverence, lest he seem to cut the union by the number — clearly denying, after the union, the whole existence itself, to speak properly, of the natures from which [0513] Christ is.

⟦q15.1|12⟧ *A more natural treatment of number.*

⟦q15.2|1+2⟧ And they suffer this, as it seems, from not knowing that every number, by its own principle, neither divides nor is divided, nor is it at all productive either of division or of union. ⟦q15.2|3⟧ For it is not of a nature to act or to be acted upon, not being an essence or an accident, about which acting and being acted upon are; but only indicative of the quantity of the subject things, however they may happen to be disposed according to relation, whether united or distinguished. ⟦q15.2|4⟧ It is, for instance — if I may make bold to say something — a name of quantity, significative without the relation that attaches to it. ⟦q15.2|5⟧ For neither does number of necessity make the natural distinction of beings, or their quantity; nor does it of itself produce the continuity of things conjoined according to the same species; nor indeed is it universally characteristic of any property of the individuals under the same species that are distinguished from one another by accidents. ⟦q15.2|6⟧ For to do these things is proper to the divine wisdom and power, which constituted the universe and keeps each thing unconfused by the natural boundaries of the beings throughout the universe; but only, as I said, does number make an indication of the quantity of things simply, and not of their how, as a name fitted to quantity.

⟦q15.3|1⟧ *On difference and quantity more exactly, and on their pious confession.*

⟦q15.3|1⟧ If, then, according to the Fathers, number is indicative of the quantity of things but not of their relation; and every quantity contemplated in natural otherness wholly admits difference; and those too who fight against the truth say there is a natural difference in Christ after the union: then they must also say, after the union, the quantity of the things that differ — without which it is impossible for difference to be — together with the number that indicates it, without which it is impossible for quantity to be known at all; if they wish to show the account of their own doctrine coherent according to well-ordered sequence. ⟦q15.3|2⟧ But if they deny the quantity of the natures preserved in Christ after the union, let them not dare to speak even of the difference of the natures from which Christ is after the union; knowing that whatever has difference in any respect whatever cannot be one according to every principle and mode. ⟦q15.3|3⟧ And what cannot be one according to every principle and mode is clearly, according to some principle and mode, two at the least, or more. ⟦q15.3|4⟧ Therefore those who say there is difference in Christ after the union cannot say that Christ is one according to every principle and mode after the union. ⟦q15.3|5⟧ But if Christ is not one according to every principle and mode after the union, clearly, according to some principle and mode, Christ is wholly two after the union. ⟦q15.3|6⟧ And if Christ is two according to some principle and mode after the union, because of the difference in him of the natures from which and in which he is, as the argument has shown, it is clear [0516] that they are impious to no purpose who say the difference of the natures from which Christ is after the union, but plainly destroy by silence, after the union, the natures themselves whose difference it is: for the denial of the things that are and are preserved is truly destruction. ⟦q15.3|7⟧ For either, by their account, the natures have not been destroyed after the union, and they both are and are preserved, and it is just that they be confessed as preserved after the union; or, if it is not just that they be confessed after the union, it is just, by their account, to say that they neither are nor are preserved in Christ, and they have been destroyed. ⟦q15.3|8⟧ For what it is not just at all to name, it is much better, as I think, and more just, to say does not exist at all either. ⟦q15.3|8⟧ Or let them persuade us by argument how it is just that the things from which Christ is, which are and are preserved after the union without any change and alteration whatever, and without conversion into one another according to the essential principle, should not be confessed in Christ; and we readily acquiesce in the privation of the confession of the things preserved and being in Christ.

⟦q15.3|9⟧ And just as Christ is not one according to every principle and mode after the union, so again Christ is not two according to every principle and mode after the union. ⟦q15.3|10+11⟧ And if Christ is not two according to every principle and mode after the union, then wholly according to some principle and mode — because of the hypostatic identity, that is, the one hypostasis, according to which he cannot have difference — Christ is one. ⟦q15.3|10+11⟧ Since, then, the same is one and two according to one principle and mode and another, it is necessary to consider according to what principle, and according to what, the same is one and two.

⟦q15.3|10+11⟧ *That it is blasphemous to say that Christ is simply one nature.*

⟦q15.4|1+2⟧ If, then, they will say that he is one according to the principle of nature, the whole will clearly, by their account, be one nature simply, without any difference whatever. ⟦q15.4|3⟧ And if we allow them to say this, Christ will be, by their account, neither God nor indeed man, neither creature at all nor Creator. ⟦q15.4|4⟧ For a nature spoken of simply admits, once for all, no signification whatever of either of these. ⟦q15.4|5⟧ But if, fleeing the manifest absurdity, they should say that Christ is one nature, but composite, they have truly not known how the gloom of impiety has thrust them into the last error.

⟦q15.5|1⟧ *On composite nature more exactly, and on the principle of it; and that those who say that Christ is one composite nature are impious.*

⟦q15.5|2⟧ For every composite nature, first, …

⟦q16.1|1⟧ [0517] …has without choice the concourse of its parts with one another in the composition.

⟦q16.1|2⟧ Then, too, it possesses its parts contemporaneous with one another and with itself, co-existing according to their coming-to-be into being, neither part pre-existing the other in time.

⟦q16.1|3⟧ And further still, it is known to have been made for the completion of the totality that magnificently outlines the universe; none of which is at all contemplated in Christ by those who wish to be pious; lest we make the concourse of the Word with the flesh involuntary and without choice; and define either God the Word as contemporaneous with the flesh according to the coming-to-be into being, or the flesh as co-eternal with the Word according to his existence without beginning; or else define Christ as having nothing more than the remaining species contemplated according to universal nature, as brought forth as a part, like the remaining species, for the completion of the universe, by the power that constituted the universe. ⟦q16.1|4+5+6⟧ For this is the definition and principle and law of every composite nature, as the power of truth and the course of logical sequence guide those who make their examination of the things that are with understanding to say. ⟦q16.1|4+5+6⟧ And who will any longer be able to be of the portion of the pious, thinking so about God and Word — him who exists before all the ages; or rather, to speak more properly, the Maker of the ages, who by volition made his self-emptying toward us men voluntary and self-chosen, and became man for the setting-right and renewal, and not for the completion, of the universe? ⟦q16.1|4+5+6⟧ For by a mode of economy, and not by a law of nature, the Word of God ineffably sojourned among men through flesh. ⟦q16.1|7⟧ Christ, then, is not a composite nature, according to the innovation of those who empty the Gospel, being altogether unenslaved to the law of composite nature as to the mode of coming into being; but a composite hypostasis, which has no composite nature whatever predicated of it by species: which is indeed paradoxical, to behold a composite hypostasis without the composite nature predicated of it by species.

⟦q16.1|8⟧ But if, according to those very men, Christ is a composite nature — the wonderful acropolis of Severus's arguments — it is altogether either generic or singular. ⟦q16.1|9⟧ For to conceive another between these is impossible. ⟦q16.1|10⟧ And if Christ is a generic nature, he will clearly be predicated of many things, and of things differing in number; and he will be in thought alone, in those in which he has his existence, and not known by himself in some hypostasis of his own, apart from the accidents contemplated in addition in the individuals under him. ⟦q16.1|11⟧ For such is the definition and principle of every generic nature; and a multitude of Christs has been brought in upon us instead of one, having in no mode any identity whatever with God or with men, through Severus's excessive and unrestrained madness against the truth. ⟦q16.1|12⟧ But if Christ is a singular nature — to pass over saying that there is in no way at all a nature circumscribed by one person, even if they put forward, as a last resort, the fabled bird, [0520] the phoenix, as something great and unassailable, an example establishing their own opinion; against which I am even afraid to say anything, lest among the understanding I incur the laughter that folly earns, trying to show the myth to be a myth. ⟦q16.1|13⟧ For if the phoenix is a bird, it is altogether also an animal. ⟦q16.1|14⟧ And if an animal, first, it is not wholly singular, inasmuch as it is an animal. ⟦q16.1|15+16+17+18⟧ Then, if an animal, it is also an ensouled, sentient body. ⟦q16.1|15+16+17+18⟧ And if the phoenix is an ensouled, sentient body, it is clearly also subject to coming-to-be and corruption. ⟦q16.1|15+16+17+18⟧ Let us ask the wise beholders of the things that are themselves whether it is possible for any of the ensouled and sentient bodies subject to coming-to-be and corruption to be of a nature singular according to hypostasis — bodies whose succession from one another according to species is the conspicuous character and definition of their being. ⟦q16.1|15+16+17+18⟧ As the divine Scripture also testifies, furnishing of itself certainty to the account, when it introduces God commanding these things to the great Noah: And God said to Noah, Enter thou and all thy house into the ark, for I have seen thee righteous before me in this generation. ⟦q16.1|19+20⟧ And of all the clean cattle bring in to thee seven and seven, male and female; and of the cattle that are not clean, two and two, male and female; and of all the clean birds of heaven, seven and seven, male and female, and of all the birds of heaven that are not clean, two and two, male and female, to keep seed alive upon all the earth. [n: Gen. vii, 1-3.] Among which, if the phoenix, as a winged animal, is numbered, it is not of a nature singular according to hypostasis, by the divine declaration. ⟦q16.1|19+20⟧ But, as I said, let us not say these things — for to fight over things not necessary is not dear to us: it is plain to all that, if Christ is a singular nature, he is consubstantial neither with God and Father nor with men. ⟦q16.1|21⟧ For what is wholly singular according to nature is altogether estranged from natural kinship with anything else whatever. ⟦q16.1|22⟧ But if, according to those very men, as the account has shown, Christ is wholly estranged according to nature from all the things that are, we shall urge the new dogmatists themselves to say what he is according to nature, and on what account they worship Christ, who, so far as the premises of their arguments go, is blasphemed by them as being nothing.

⟦q16.1|22⟧ [0521] If, then, a great and fearful danger of necessity follows, according to the force of the account rendered, for those who say that Christ is one according to nature, that is, one nature, whether called simple or composite, and this either generic, that is, common, or singular, that is, specific, let us with all vigilance keep our hearts pure of such an opinion, that we may also be set free from the threat that awaits it. ⟦q16.1|23⟧ And let us piously confess the "two" in Christ according to the principle of natural otherness, that is, of the difference of the natures from which he is, preserved even after the union, as of parts in a whole; according to which the parts never naturally coincide with one another, so long as each keeps unimpaired its own principle of nature relative to the other; and the "one" according to the principle of hypostatic identity, according to which Christ, as a whole, admits no distinction at all from difference with respect to himself, that is, no partition, so long as he is believed to be one hypostasis both before the flesh and after the incarnation, and is worshipped and glorified by us as one of the holy and royal Trinity. ⟦q16.1|24⟧ For Christ's hypostasis is singular, as a whole, divided out to the extremes on no account, according to its characteristic property, by which it is marked off from them. ⟦q16.1|25⟧ By extremes I now mean God and Father, from whom Christ has been begotten divinely before the ages, and the holy, all-glorious Virgin and Mother, from whom the same has been born humanly for our sake — the hypostasis keeping undiminished, by the kinship of its own parts with both the extremes, the identity according to essence; in which parts Christ, admitting the principle of difference, does not depart from his own singular hypostasis. ⟦q16.1|26⟧ For every totality, and especially one by composition, contemplated from different things, while singularly keeping the identity of its own hypostasis, also has the unconfused difference of its own parts with one another, according to which it preserves unadulterated the principle according to essence of each part relative to the other. ⟦q16.1|27⟧ And likewise the parts too, in their composition with one another, keeping pure and undiminished the principle according to nature, have the unitary, undistinguished identity of their own totality, according to which they singularly keep wholly undivided the principle according to hypostasis.

⟦q16.1|27⟧ If, then, as the account, running with truth, has shown, it is altogether impious in Christ to decree that the "one" lies in the principle according to nature, as, conversely, the "two" in the principle according to hypostasis — for the one is proper to the stench of Apollinarius, who smears together and confounds the natures into one nature, and makes the economy wholly indiscernible, positing that properly no nature exists after the union. [0524] For each of these escapes being what it is, as they pass over and fall into one another. ⟦q16.1|28⟧ And the other is proper to the Jewish derangement of Nestorius, who partitions the one hypostasis into two hypostases, and denies the whole economy, refusing to say that God the Word was united to flesh according to hypostasis, essentially. ⟦q16.1|28⟧ We, for our part, let us use our speech rightly and fittingly, turning the expressions of the perverse to what is straight: the "two" according to the principle of natural otherness, that is, of difference; the "one" according to the principle of the hypostasis; that both the differentness of the natures from which Christ is may be kept in Christ after the union, and the sameness and singleness of the hypostasis of God the Word incarnate may likewise be preserved unmultiplied. ⟦q16.1|29⟧ For the reciprocal indication and confession of the realities, as the heretics have it, works the obliteration of the subjects; since the truth does not at all endure to allow the proper appellations of the realities to be exchanged for one another. ⟦q16.1|30⟧ For this reason we confess Christ two natures, as I said, according to the principle of the difference of the natures from which he is, preserved in him even after the union, as being the same God and man after the union.

⟦q16.2|1⟧ *That he who calls Christ God and man after the union, having chosen to believe rightly, of necessity confesses together with the names the natures after the union.*

⟦q16.2|2⟧ For if Christ is God and man after the union, according to the common confession, clearly, having the realities, he makes the names true after the union. ⟦q16.2|3⟧ For realities are conceived before their own names; and there could never at any time be a name without the reality, which of itself provides the name with its imposition and receives in return its signification. ⟦q16.2|4⟧ But if Christ does not have in the union the realities of which the names are, he will be shown, on their account, to have the appellations bare, as lying with nothing, and empty of realities; and Christ will be, according to them, God and man in name only, and not in reality: than which what could be more godless? ⟦q16.2|5⟧ But if, refusing to be so mad, they should say that in Christ the appellations are not empty of realities after the union, then, just as they do not refuse to speak of the two appellations of Christ after the union, so let them also speak, after the union, of the two natures of Christ, from which and in which he subsists, of which the appellations also are — if in truth they faithfully confess that the things united are preserved after the union really, according to nature.

⟦q16.3|1⟧ *On the expression "In two"; and on the one nature of the Word incarnate, a pious confession according to the Fathers; and a denial of the one composite nature.*

⟦q16.3|2⟧ And again we believe the same to be in two natures without division, as in Godhead and manhood. ⟦q16.3|3⟧ For just as, saying that Christ is from two natures, we understand him to be from Godhead and manhood, as a whole from parts, so too, saying "in two natures" after…

⟦q17.1|1⟧ [0525] …the union, we believe him to be in Godhead and manhood, as a whole in parts. ⟦q17.1|2⟧ Now the parts of Christ are his Godhead and his manhood, from which and in which he subsists. ⟦q17.1|3⟧ And again we have been taught to believe, to say and to proclaim one nature of God the Word incarnate, in flesh having an intellectual and rational soul, according to the holy Cyril; understanding, by the saying "incarnate", that the essence of our nature has been brought in. ⟦q17.1|4⟧ And again, of one and the same Christ both the miracles and the passions; and of the same the two births, the one before the ages, bodilessly, from the Father, and the one in time, bodily, from a Mother and Virgin, accomplished for our sake. ⟦q17.1|5⟧ Therefore above all, and in truth, we confess her properly Theotokos, as having given birth to God the Word, begotten of the Father before all the ages, incarnate from her in the last times.

⟦q17.1|6⟧ But we altogether refuse to say that Christ is one composite nature: not only for the reasons stated, but because no one at all of the approved Fathers has appeared up to now to have spoken this expression, and as being manifestly the offspring of Apollinarius the heretic and of his disciples; whose error Severus having accepted, he fittingly also decks himself out in their expressions. ⟦q17.1|7⟧ For Christ is not a natural monad by composition, as Apollinarius and Severus have it, so long as he keeps preserved, even after the union, the unconfused otherness of the natures from which he is. ⟦q17.1|8⟧ Nor yet a hypostatic dyad, as Nestorius has it, so long as the things that came together do not subsist by themselves and wholly distinguished from one another; and so long as God the Word incarnate is one even after the incarnation, and assumed the flesh united to him, rationally and intellectually ensouled, which had not pre-subsisted by the proper principle of its nature; but one composite hypostasis, according to the Fathers, according to which the same is both wholly God, and one of the holy and all-praised Trinity, with the manhood, because of the Godhead; and the same wholly man, and one of men, with the Godhead, because of the manhood.

⟦q17.2|1⟧ *On the composite hypostasis, a more natural treatment; and an exact demonstration that those who confess Christ one composite hypostasis do not fall into the same absurdities as those who call him one composite nature.*

⟦q17.2|2⟧ But if anyone should think that, because every composite hypostasis too possesses its parts contemporaneous with one another, the same absurdity is concluded also for those who say that Christ is one composite hypostasis, such a one, by my account, does not suppose rightly. ⟦q17.2|3⟧ For not simply every composite hypostasis has its parts contemporaneous with one another in coming-to-be, but that which ranks, clearly, under a composite nature, and has such a species predicated of it; which, having [0528] the aggregate of the idioms about it interwoven with the common and universal of the essence or nature predicated of it, is a nature, that is, with idioms. ⟦q17.2|4⟧ If, then, the nature under which it ranks, and the species predicated, is composite, it too must altogether be composite, and possess its parts contemporaneous with one another, because of the essence, that is, the nature, predicated of it, which so exists as to be the same as it in species, apart from its distinguishing marks that mark it off. ⟦q17.2|5⟧ For just as, defining the hypostasis spoken of simply, we say: Hypostasis is an essence with idioms; or, an essence comprehensive of the particulars, of all the idioms in its own individual; so, defining the hypostasis spoken of with a qualification, and not simply, we say: A composite hypostasis is a composite essence with idioms; or, a composite essence comprehensive of the particulars, of all the idioms in its own individual. ⟦q17.2|6⟧ For what is contemplated in common in the individuals under the same species characterizes primarily the generic of the essence or nature in the individuals under it. ⟦q17.2|7⟧ But the composite is common to all the individuals under the composite species; therefore the composite primarily characterizes a composite nature in the individuals under it, and not a hypostasis.

⟦q17.2|8⟧ And again, besides, every definition of the individuals that rank under them is from the common and universal; and all the things under the generic naturally have from them the beginning of their own description, so that it may be clear what the thing defined properly and primarily is. ⟦q17.2|9⟧ But if this is true, as indeed it is, it is plain that he who has called composite a hypostasis referred to a nature has declared, together with the individual of the hypostasis, what inheres generically in the common of the essence, with the idioms that characterize it specifically. ⟦q17.2|10⟧ So that he who has said that a hypostasis ranking under some nature is composite has said nothing other than a composite nature, that is, essence, with idioms. ⟦q17.2|11⟧ If, then, as has been shown, every composite nature has without choice the concourse of its own parts with one another in the composition, and possesses its parts contemporaneous with one another, and has come to be for the completion of the totality contemplated in the things that are, which makes up the universe species by species;
