then clearly every hypostasis too, according to what is natural, has a constitution unto being the same as, and invariant with, its own genus and species. ⟦q17.2|12⟧ Let them show, then, that Christ's composite hypostasis ranks under a composite nature, and let them conclude that it possesses its parts contemporaneous, and let them justly lay the same blame against us. ⟦q17.2|13⟧ But so long as they cannot do this, they are setting the props of their own doctrines upon sand. ⟦q17.2|14⟧ For he who ignorantly forces Christ under these aforesaid absurdities, because he both is a composite hypostasis and is piously confessed so by us, has wandered far from the road that leads to the truth — or rather, to speak more properly, from the truth itself. ⟦q17.2|15⟧ For this great and venerable mystery of Christ [0529] neither has, as an individual, some universal and generic nature predicated of it as a species; nor indeed is it itself a genus or a species predicated of the individuals naturally under it, so as to be able to be brought under any of the rules stated; inasmuch as it has the concurrence of God the Word with the flesh in the composition not equal and like to that of composite things in their concourse with one another.

⟦q17.3|1⟧ *That only the union of the Word with the flesh has come about by assumption, since he pre-existed and willed to be emptied unto union with flesh.*

⟦q17.3|2+3⟧ For in the case of all things universally that are composite according to nature, it is not the concourse of this with that by assumption that makes the constitution of this species, as of a whole from parts; but the simultaneous coming-to-be of the parts from non-being into being, by their concourse with one another at the same time as their being, makes the composition of the whole. ⟦q17.3|4⟧ But the mystery of our Lord and God and Saviour Jesus Christ did not come about in this mode; but being God by nature, and by nature Son of God, simple and bodiless, and co-eternal with the Father, and Creator of all the ages, willing, by a wish of love for man, he became man by assumption of flesh; and for our sake, being simple according to nature and bodiless, he became from us, by way of economy, composite according to hypostasis and embodied, as the holy and great Dionysius the Areopagite says, under divine inspiration, in the first chapter of his treatise *On the Divine Names*, going through the matter of the divine incarnation thus: « And man-loving pre-eminently, in that it has wholly shared, in truth, in what is ours, in one of its hypostases, calling back to itself and raising up the human extremity, from which [ineffably] the simple Jesus was composed; and the eternal has taken on extension in time; and he has come to be within our nature who superessentially transcends every order according to nature. »

⟦q17.3|4⟧ If, then, not according to the law and order of the nature of composite things, but by another ordinance, contrary to the nature of composite things — for by assumption the Word of God was inexpressibly composed with the flesh, and not by taking being in a coming-to-be together with the flesh unto composition, for the completion of some whole according to species, as the word of truth piercingly cries out to all — then in vain do the ignorant lawlessly attempt to bring under the ordinances that are under nature the composition that goes beyond every definition and principle of nature. ⟦q17.3|5⟧ For this reason he who says that the becoming man came about by assumption of flesh both preserves the existence of God the Word before the ages, and piously confesses his voluntary and self-chosen incarnation in time; and keeps unconfused, even after [0532] the union, the difference between the Word himself who assumed and the flesh assumed. ⟦q17.3|6⟧ But he who does not confess that the Word of God became man without change by assumption of flesh will be able in no mode truly to confess any of the things stated. ⟦q17.3|7+8⟧ For how, if the Word did not pre-exist without beginning, did he by volition assume flesh different according to essence? — in whose case above all, as I think, the union of what is of another nature is properly said to have come about by assumption; since he alone, and alone in this manner, impassibly and truly assumed what is of another essence, and kept himself unchangeable and unmultiplied in every principle and mode, and what was assumed unalterable: which is impossible to come about in the case of originated nature, in which every composition naturally comes about at the same time as the coming-to-be of the parts, for the completion of some whole according to species, by their simultaneous concurrence with one another, keeping equal, throughout the whole, the proportion of the parts to one another; as is the case with us, and with whatever things have had it as their lot to have a composite nature according to species. ⟦q17.3|9⟧ For in us the soul has the natural powers of the body proportioned to its own operations, the body being receptive according to nature because of the soul's simultaneous coming-to-be into being together with the flesh. ⟦q17.3|10+11⟧ But the Word of God, having in no principle or mode the powers of the nature assumed by him proportioned to his own operations according to nature — for what is above nature is not measured by nature, nor is there anything at all among the things that are receptive of him according to nature — therefore, alone of all, by assumption of flesh rationally and intellectually ensouled, willing, he became man ineffably, as being and pre-being and able for all things, having innovated the natures by the mode above nature, that he might save man.

⟦q17.3|12⟧ This faith I have learned, and in it I have been taught, both by our holy and blessed Fathers who have gone before, and by those now living who have been entrusted with the helm of the catholic holy Church of God and steer her rightly to the harbour of the divine will; with which, by their supplications, I shall also depart, leaving the present life, offering to God, in place of every merit, this confession, undefiled and unstained, and higher than every heretical storm. ⟦q17.3|13⟧ For I cannot boast in a life made splendid with works of righteousness, having become throughout all my life here a self-chosen transgressor of the divine laws.

⟦q17.3|14⟧ These things, then, I have written to you, as far as was possible, in summary, my blessed lords, so that you may know, if there is anything useful in what has been written, how you may at least in some measure send away the wolves clothed in the fleece, who terrify with their meaningless barking the divine and gentle sheep of Christ's holy flock, and may not be swept away by the falsehood that maliciously feigns truth. ⟦q17.3|14⟧ But the sayings of the Fathers that plead for the Church's faith I have not yet been able to put into the letter, because of [0533] the great want of books with me. ⟦q17.3|15⟧ For you have there my blessed master, father and teacher, the lord abba Sophronius, truly sober-minded, and a wise advocate of the truth, and an unconquered champion of the divine doctrines, able in deed and word to contend against every heresy, and, with all his other good things, rich in a multitude of divine books, and eagerly enriching those who wish to learn divine things. ⟦q17.3|16⟧ Resorting to him, then, you will receive, I know well, all the right and unstumbling knowledge of the divine and saving doctrines. ⟦q17.3|17⟧ Farewell.

⟦q18.1|1⟧ LETTER 14

⟦q18.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same, a dogmatic letter.*

⟦q18.1|2⟧ God, being good according to nature — nothing of what he naturally does is evil. ⟦q18.1|3⟧ But man, becoming good by diligence of inclination, is always shown rejoicing in the things God naturally does. ⟦q18.1|4⟧ For man has no other discernment of his being good than only his co-disposition toward what is done by God. ⟦q18.1|5⟧ For the making of good things is proper to God; to man, the co-disposition toward them. ⟦q18.1|6⟧ For a man's virtue is to show that what has been made by God is cherished by the disposition of his inclination. ⟦q18.1|7⟧ So then, if everything that has God as the cause of its own coming-to-be is good, then good, with reason, and the greatest of goods, is the union of those who stood apart in faith, since it is manifestly the work of God. ⟦q18.1|8⟧ And if this is altogether true, I know that you rejoice, beholding the scattered being gathered into one, being good as you are, through your co-disposition toward what is noble.

⟦q18.1|9⟧ I beg you, then, God-guarded ones, to receive as good men the bearer of this my modest note, the most God-loving deacon, my lord [0536] Cosmas, a man noble and understanding, and adorned with the manners that are according to God, and to enrol him among your own friends; and, if there should be need to lay a request on his behalf before the God-honoured pope, that he may recover his own rank of the diaconate in the Church in which he had been enrolled, not to shrink from it. ⟦q18.1|10⟧ For so genuinely has he accepted the pious account of the divine doctrines of the Church, as even to champion it ardently, and to know this as the definition and ordinance of piety: never to have refused to hold any of the things that Christ is after the inexpressible union, being at once, in the same respect, truly the same God and man. ⟦q18.1|11⟧ For he did not cast off what he was, becoming truly what he was not; for he is unchangeable. ⟦q18.1|12⟧ Nor did he alter what he became, remaining what he was; for he loves man. ⟦q18.1|12⟧ But he is wholly both, naturally, according to hypostasis, as one from both, and through his one self preserving both without change and diminution, as being truly these by union — the union according to hypostasis. ⟦q18.1|13⟧ For the union according to hypostasis, having its contradistinction against division, and not against the natural difference of the things united according to it, makes a complete abolition of the one and works a firm preservation of the other; of which number is the indication. ⟦q18.1|14⟧ Since no one could ever conceive a difference wholly without share in quantity, even were he the wisest in arguments and the hardest to fight.

⟦q18.1|15⟧ For so long as the parts of the whole by composition, from which the whole is constituted, are preserved without confusion, the natural difference to one another of the parts of the whole that are preserved by union is not unknown. ⟦q18.1|16⟧ And so long as the principle of the difference according to essence of the parts remains preserved in the whole, it is impossible that the quantity of the parts of the whole that differ from one another according to essence should be unknown — of which number naturally makes an indication, and not a division; because number is indicative not of the relation of realities, that is, of union or division, but only of quantity simply, and not of quantity in some manner. ⟦q18.1|17⟧ But if number naturally makes an indication of quantity and not of relation, he who piously uses number after the union only for the indication of the difference, preserved in Christ, of the natures from which he is, does not divide the union at all, but knows only that the natures have remained unconfused after the union; from which the one Christ and Lord, and Son and God, exists — incarnate, clearly, and perfectly become man. ⟦q18.1|18⟧ Therefore he also upholds after the union one nature of the Word himself incarnate, in flesh having the intellectual and rational soul, not ignoring the difference, but removing and, as it were, setting outside the division; that at once, through these very expressions, he may drive [0537] the confusion of Apollinarius and the division of Nestorius, evils diametrically opposed, from the divine court, and may become a pure worshipper of the mystery: confessing the same begotten from above, from the Father, before all the ages, and the same born below — by assumption, clearly, of flesh rationally and intellectually ensouled — from his mother, for our sake, in the last times. ⟦q18.1|19⟧ And for this reason believing the holy Virgin to be properly and in truth Theotokos, since, having conceived the Word himself in place of seed, she both carried and bore him, incarnate from her: the same operating the miracles; the same enduring by volition the experience of human sufferings; the same crucified, buried, risen on the third day according to the Scriptures; taken up incarnate into the heavens, from which, without flesh, he came down to us, as he himself knows; he who, as God according to nature, has no passage from places to places. ⟦q18.1|20⟧ For by nature he is infinite, and for this reason in no mode contained by the things that are, as being free of all circumscription; and by nature loving to man, and for this reason enduring by volition our fleshly birth, and becoming containable by what is ours: the same who will come with his own glory to judge living and dead, and to give to each the just distribution of the things prepared according to worth.

⟦q18.1|20⟧ The aforesaid most God-loving lord Cosmas having gladly accepted this confession, I too accepted him rejoicing, and, having come to know his simple disposition according to Christ, I preferred him to my own soul; and on the dogmatic chapters about which he asked, I have given him a concise answer in writing, which it is fitting that you too, God-guarded ones, should take and exercise. ⟦q18.1|21⟧ For you benefit him by your fine exposition of the text, filling up the sense of what was left out through brevity. ⟦q18.1|21⟧ And so much for this.

⟦q18.1|22+23⟧ I remind you, God-guarded ones, to watch and to pray, according to the Lord's command, that we may not be caught in the snares of the temptations that stand around us on every side. [n: Matth. xxvi, 41.] For if we watch and are sober, I know that we guard ourselves against the wiles of the demons; and if we pray, we draw the divine grace to our help, fighting alongside us and showing us victors over every opposing power, [0540] and delivering us from all error and ignorance. ⟦q18.1|22+23⟧ And this above all we ought to do now, when, even without the commandment, nature becomes the teacher of refuge in God, taking distress as its counsellor. ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ For what is more distressing than the evils that now encompass the inhabited world? ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ What more dreadful to those who perceive what is happening? ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ What more pitiable or more fearful to those who suffer? ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ To see a nation of the desert and barbarous overrunning another's land as if it were its own; and the gentle commonwealth being consumed by wild and untamed beasts that have only the bare shape of human form; and the Jewish people, rejoicing from of old in the blood of men, and knowing as the only divine good pleasure the murder of the creature; and for this reason raving the more, so as to become more conspicuous in the wealth of wickedness than all those notorious for vice; and thinking to serve God by the things God abhors; the only people of all the peoples on earth most faithless, and for this reason most fit for the reception of the opposing power; going before in procession, in every manner and disposition, of the sojourn of the evil one; and by what it does announcing the coming of the Antichrist, since it failed to know that of him who is truly Saviour; the hostile and lawless, hating man and hating God, and for this reason the more man-hating because God-hating, and permitted to revel in outrages against the saints, since the vengeance is near, that it may be punished the more justly, once its tyranny and insurrection against God have been made perfectly manifest through the facts; the people that is the patron of falsehood, the craftsman of bloodshed and the enemy of truth; the bitter persecutor of my faith, through which the polytheistic error was dissolved and the phalanxes of the demons were driven away. ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ Through which All the nations have clapped their hands, according to the prophecy, and shouted to God with a voice of exultation; for the Lord is most high, fearful, a great King over all the earth. [n: Psal. xlvi, 2, 3.] The only-begotten Son, sojourning through flesh, was known to them mystically: Lord, as enjoining virtue, as on servants, on those who hold to the commandments through practice; most high, as provider of true knowledge to those who, by longing for wisdom, press on through contemplation to the apprehension of the mysteries bare of symbols; fearful, as judge and avenger of transgressions; king, as distributor to each of the things prepared according to worth; and [0541] great, as doing contraries through contraries above nature, and fashioning dispassion through passion and life through death, and by a paradoxical power, through his own privations according to the flesh, bestowing on nature the unchangeable habits of good things. ⟦q18.1|24+25+26+27⟧ And not even so is the senseless people ashamed, and does it cease persecuting faith and virtue. ⟦q18.1|28+29⟧ Which it plainly persecutes through envy, as having fallen from both and not knowing that it has fallen, limping on both hams, and wholly unable to rise up from the fall of impiety — or unwilling, to speak more fittingly; as ever betraying faith and virtue for the sake of pride and pleasure, as an apostate and foolish people and an undisciplined nation. [n: Deut. xxxii, 6.] Between which having divided his life, the apostate, full of lawless deeds, makes the one the mother of apostasy from God, the other the craftsman of hatred of man; so that both God and creation are outraged by him: the one despised, the other corrupted by the defilements of his practices.

⟦q18.1|30⟧ What of these things, as I said, is more fearful to the eyes or ears of Christians? ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ A nation cruel and outlandish, permitted to lift up its hands in wrath against the divine inheritance. ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ But the multitude of our sins has made these things happen. ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ For we have not lived worthily of the Gospel of Christ. [n: Philipp. i, 27.] All of us have sinned, all have done lawlessly, [n: Dan. ix, 5.] all have abandoned the way of the commandments that said, I am the way; [n: Joan. xiv, 6.] and we have become beasts toward one another, not knowing the grace of love for man, and the mystery of the sufferings of God incarnate for us; and we have travelled pathless tracks, [n: Sap. v, 7.] ratifying, that is, by the disposition of our inclination, the relations to the passions of dishonour. [n: Rom. i, 26.] But let us watch and pray, lest our hearts be weighed down the more in surfeiting and drunkenness. [n: Luc. xxi, 34.] For surfeiting is the doubting about the word of faith in times of persecution; and drunkenness the casting away of the natural thoughts, through which the true discernment of the things that are naturally comes about, whenever from slackness the soul gives way under the assaults of temptations, and, darkened, suffers a tossing about the word, as I said, of faith. ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ For if we pray and watch, we hold the more firmly our faith in our Lord and God Jesus Christ, beholding, and receiving by experience, the outcome of what he foretold; and we are not at all astonished, nor do we undergo a tossing in soul, as suffering contrary to the promise, but we hold the faith more firmly, seeing the Lord's prediction fulfilled through the facts. ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ But if the outcome of what was foretold is wont to make a confirmation of faith, the more through what is happening [0544] we too shall confirm our faith, finding him who spoke to be true; and let us confess him God before men, dreading no death at all, that he too may confess us before the Father, [n: Luc. xii, 8.] and bring us, saved, through the good confession, which he himself began as a pattern of our good things, having borne witness before Pontius Pilate and confessed the good confession; [n: I Tim. vi, 12.] which may we be counted worthy to imitate, by his grace, whenever the time should call; not rashly leaping upon the dangers for the word of piety, but fleeing temptations as far as we can, both out of sparing for our persecutors and by the testing, according to full knowledge, of the natural weakness present in us; educating those who are infants, and awaiting the Word who calls; who, invisibly present in the contests of those who lawfully fight for piety, runs through the course of martyrdom, as he alone who is able to conquer sense and nature; since martyrdom is plainly a victory over sense and nature, through which there naturally comes about an insurrection of mind and reason against the passions, by which the longing for God is made firm, persuading its lovers, paradoxically, to cross over through death to the one longed for: before whom may we be counted worthy to stand, flashing with our confessions toward him, which may we also carry away with us as we depart hence and pass over to the endless world, by the prayers and intercessions of our all-holy Lady, the Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary, and of all the saints. ⟦q18.1|31+32+33+34+35+36+37+38⟧ Amen.

⟦q18.2|1⟧ *Scholion.*

⟦q18.3|1⟧ 1. ⟦q18.3|2⟧ He who uses the expression of the two natures, he says, only for the indication of the difference in Christ, fights Apollinarius; and he again who speaks the expression of the one nature of God the Word incarnate casts out the division of Nestorius. ⟦q18.3|3⟧ For he who flees equally the impiety of each of these speaks the two expressions.

⟦q19.1|1⟧ LETTER 15

⟦q19.1|1⟧ *Of the same, on common and proper, that is, essence and hypostasis, to Cosmas, the most God-beloved deacon of Alexandria.*

⟦q19.1|2⟧ To the most God-beloved lord Cosmas, deacon, Maximus the lowly, greeting.

⟦q19.1|3⟧ Since much discussion has been set in motion among us concerning the divine incarnation, and thou, touching upon the very subtle doctrines of piety themselves and taking occasion from them, didst ask my lowliness to say, man of God, what the common and universal is, and what the particular and proper, so that from this the whole account of the union might become clear to us: I shall say nothing at all of my own; but what I was taught by the Fathers, that I state, altering nothing of their teaching on these matters.

⟦q19.1|3⟧ [0545] The common and universal, then, that is the generic, according to the Fathers, is the essence and the nature; for they say that these are the same as each other. ⟦q19.1|4+5+6⟧ The proper and particular is the hypostasis and the person; for according to them these are the same as each other. ⟦q19.1|4+5+6⟧ And the great Basil shows this, writing as follows to Terentius: «But if we too must say briefly what seems right to us, we shall say this: that the ratio which the common has to the proper, this the essence has to the hypostasis. ⟦q19.1|4+5+6⟧ For each of us both partakes of being by the common principle of the essence, and by the idioms about him is such a one or such a one.» ⟦q19.1|4+5+6⟧ And again the same, going through such matters with Amphilochius, says this: «Essence and hypostasis have this difference, which the common has toward what is particular to each. ⟦q19.1|4+5+6⟧ For example, as the living being has toward such a man.» ⟦q19.1|7⟧ And after other things: «But those who say that essence and hypostasis are the same are compelled to confess only different persons.» ⟦q19.1|7⟧ And again, teaching a certain canoness, he wrote, interpreting the sense of the homoousion: «And this word also sets right the evil of Sabellius. ⟦q19.1|8⟧ For it removes the identity of the hypostasis, and introduces the perfect notion of the persons; for a thing is not itself consubstantial with itself, but one thing with another. ⟦q19.1|9⟧ So that it stands well and piously, both marking off the property of the hypostases and presenting the invariance of the nature.» ⟦q19.1|9⟧ And again, in the second to the Neocaesareans, he teaches, saying: «For one must also know that, as he who does not confess the common of the essence falls into polytheism, so he who does not grant the proper things of the hypostases is carried away into Judaism.» ⟦q19.1|10⟧ And again, in what he made Eustathius the Armenian sign, he is seen writing expressly: «It is fitting, then, that they confess plainly that they believe according to the words set forth by our Fathers at Nicaea, and according to the sense soundly conveyed by the words. ⟦q19.1|11⟧ For there are some who even in this faith falsify the word of truth, and drag the meaning of the words in it toward their own wish. ⟦q19.1|12⟧ Seeing that Marcellus too, being impious toward the hypostasis of our Lord Jesus Christ and expounding him as a bare word, dared to pretend that he had taken his starting-points from there, expounding badly the sense of the homoousion. ⟦q19.1|13⟧ And some of those from the impiety of the Libyan Sabellius, supposing hypostasis and essence to be the same, draw from there the occasions for the construction of their own blasphemy, from its being written in the creed: *But if anyone says that the Son of God is from another essence or hypostasis, him the holy and catholic Church anathematizes.* ⟦q19.1|14⟧ For they did not say that essence and hypostasis are the same. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ For if the words signified one and the same meaning, what need was there of each? ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ But it is clear that, since some denied the "from the [0548] essence", and others said "from some other hypostasis", they thus forbade both as alien to the ecclesiastical mind. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ Since, where they made their own mind plain, they said that the Son is from the essence of the Father, no longer adding "from the hypostasis". ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ So that the former is set down for the destruction of the wicked mind; the latter holds the manifestation of the saving doctrine.»

⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And in concord with this Gregory too, who is named from theology, is seen saying this in the first theological oration: «When I say the mean, I mean the truth; to which alone it is well to look, refusing both the bad contraction and the more absurd division; so that neither, the account being contracted into one hypostasis for fear of polytheism, should we be left with bare names, supposing the same one to be Father and Son and Holy Spirit.» ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And again, after a little: «Since one must both keep the one God and confess the three hypostases, that is three persons, and each with its property. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And the one God would be kept, as my account has it, the Son and the Spirit being referred to one cause, not compounded nor blended together; and the three hypostases, no blending or dissolution or confusion being conceived; lest the whole be destroyed by those things by which the One is extolled more than is fitting. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And in respect of the one and selfsame — that I may so name it — motion and purpose of the Godhead, and the identity of the essence.» ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And again in the Farewell oration: «We believe in Father and Son and Holy Spirit, consubstantial and of one glory, in whom baptism too has its perfecting — thou knowest, who hast been initiated — being a denial of godlessness and a confession of Godhead; and so we are made perfect, recognizing the One in the essence and in the indivisibility of the worship, and the Three in the hypostases, that is persons.» ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And indeed in the oration on the Lights too the same says this: «When I say God, be ye shone about by one light and by three: three according to the properties, that is hypostases, if anyone likes so to call them, or persons — for we shall not fight about the names, so long as the syllables bear toward the same notion — and one according to the principle of the essence, that is of the Godhead.» ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ So great a concord of the divine doctrines did our God-proclaiming Fathers Gregory and Basil show there to be, saying expressly that the nature is the same as the essence, as common and universal, and the person the same as the hypostasis, as specific and particular; [0549] in no way commixing the meaning of the terms through their conversion into one another and confusion. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ For, being operated upon by one and the same Spirit, they delivered to the peoples the right confession of the faith. ⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ And in concord with these thou wilt find all those also who by the grace of the Spirit were entrusted with the helm of the Church, upholding the right account of the pious faith and in no way at all turning aside from this meaning.

⟦q19.1|15+16+17+18+19+20+21+22⟧ *A more natural demonstration, presenting that none of the things that are is the same as another in essence and hypostasis; but those that are the same in essence are other in their hypostases, and those that are the same in hypostasis are wholly other in essence.*

⟦q19.2|1⟧ If essence and nature are the same, and person and hypostasis the same, it is clear that things connatural and consubstantial with one another are wholly of other hypostases from one another. ⟦q19.2|2⟧ For according to both — I mean the nature and the hypostasis — nothing among the things that are is the same as another. ⟦q19.2|3+4⟧ Because things united with one another according to one and the same nature, that is essence — that is, things being of the same essence and nature — could never be united according to one and the same hypostasis, that is person; that is, they will not be able to have one person and one hypostasis; and things united according to one and the same hypostasis, that is person, could never be conjoined according to one and the same essence, that is nature; that is, they could never appear having become of one and the same essence and nature. ⟦q19.2|5⟧ But things united according to one and the same nature, that is essence — that is, things being of one and the same essence and nature — are distinguished from one another by their hypostases, that is persons, as is the case with angels and men and all the creatures contemplated in species and genus. ⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ For angel is distinguished from angel, and man from man, and ox from ox, and dog from dog, according to the hypostasis, but not according to the nature and the essence.

⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ And the argument will dare to say the greater thing: that not even in the very first, beginningless and creative cause of the things that are do we contemplate the nature and the hypostasis as the same as each other; if indeed we know one essence and nature of Godhead, existing in three hypostases differing from one another by their properties, and three hypostases in one and the same essence and nature of the Godhead. ⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ For Monad in Trinity, and Trinity in Monad, is what is worshipped by us: Father and Son and Holy Spirit, one God; the Son not being Father, but what the Father is; the Spirit not being Son, but what the Son is. ⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ For all that the Father is, save unbegottenness, the Son is (for he is begotten); and all that the Son is, save generation, the Holy Spirit is (for it proceeds): unbegottenness and generation and [0552] procession not cutting the one nature and power of the ineffable Godhead into three unequal or equal essences and natures, but characterizing the persons, that is the hypostases, in which, or which, the one Godhead is, that is essence and nature. ⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ And things united according to one and the same hypostasis, that is person — that is, being of one hypostasis and completive of one person — differ by the principle of the essence, that is nature; as is the case with the human soul and body, and with all things that have had their concourse with one another according to hypostasis. ⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ For these are not consubstantial with one another.

⟦q19.2|6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ Things, then, united according to one and the same essence, that is nature — that is, being of one and the same essence and nature — are wholly consubstantial with one another, and of other hypostases: consubstantial, by the principle of the essential community contemplated in them invariantly in natural identity, according to which one is not more than another that which he is and is called, but all admit the one and same definition and principle of the essence; of other hypostases, by the principle of the personal otherness distinguishing them, according to which one is distinguished from another, not coinciding with one another in the characteristic idioms according to hypostasis, but each, by the aggregate of the idioms about him, bears the most specific principle of his own being according to hypostasis, according to which he does not admit communion with those connatural and consubstantial with him. ⟦q19.2|12+13⟧ But things united according to one and the same hypostasis, that is person — that is, being completive of one and the same hypostasis by union — are of one hypostasis with one another, and of other essence: of one hypostasis, by the principle of the personal undivided monad completed from them by union, according to which the idioms that divide each from the community according to essence proper to it are made, at their concourse with one another simultaneous with being, characteristic of the one hypostasis completed from them; according to which their identity toward one another is contemplated, admitting no difference whatsoever; as is the case with the human soul and body. ⟦q19.2|14⟧ For the idioms that mark off someone's body from the rest of bodies, and someone's soul from the rest of souls, running together by union, characterize and at the same time mark off from the rest of men the hypostasis completed from them — of Peter, say, or of Paul — but not the soul of Peter or Paul from its own body. ⟦q19.2|15⟧ For both, soul and body, are the same as each other by the principle of the one hypostasis completed from them by union; because neither of them subsisted by itself, separated from the other, before the composition from them toward the coming-to-be of a species. ⟦q19.2|16⟧ For at once the coming-to-be, at once the composition, at once also the completion of the species from them by composition. ⟦q19.2|17⟧ And of other essence, by the principle of the natural otherness toward one another; according to which they in no way admit [0553] one another's definitions and principles according to essence; but each renders the principle of its own essence other and other, not coinciding with the principle of the other; according to which, preserving the essential difference of soul and body, we do not confuse the hypostasis completed from them, as though it received annihilation by the conversion and change of one into the other of the parts, that is natures, of which it was composed.

⟦q19.2|18⟧ If, then, by the concourse of soul and body simultaneous toward the coming-to-be of a species, the particular man is by nature brought to completion — by the principle of the natural community of his own parts preserving his consubstantiality toward other men; by the principle of their property guarding toward the other men his being of another hypostasis; being united by the community according to nature of his own parts to the other men, toward whom he bears one and the same nature according to species; and being divided by the property of the parts according to hypostasis from the rest of men, toward whom he bears a person other and different according to hypostasis; by which principle he is distinguished from the other men, guarding the unity of his own personal undivided monad wholly without difference; and by which principle he is by nature united to the other men, preserving unconfused the difference of the essential otherness of his own parts — then clear to us from this, and having nothing riddling, has the account become of the incarnation, that is the becoming man, of the one of the holy and consubstantial and worshipful Trinity, God the Word.

⟦q19.3|1⟧ *That Christ, united to the extremes by the communities according to essence of his own parts, kept the difference of the parts toward one another preserved; and by the properties of the parts showed the identity according to hypostasis, as of a whole, common to both.*

⟦q19.3|2⟧ Teaching us expressly that the Word of God — being perfect according to the nature and the essence, according to which he is the same as the Father and the Spirit and consubstantial, and according to the person and the hypostasis, according to which he stands other toward the Father and the Spirit, preserving the personal difference unconfused — incarnate of the Holy Spirit and of the holy Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary, perfectly became man; that is, became perfect man, by the assumption, namely, of flesh having a soul intellectual and rational, which received in him both its nature and its hypostasis — that is, its being and its subsisting — at the very moment of the conception of the Word; since the Word himself, in place of seed — or rather as seed of his own incarnation — was found, willing it, and became composite according to hypostasis, he who

⟦q20.1|1⟧ [0556] is according to nature simple and incomposite; one and the same, remaining unchangeable, undivided and unconfused in the permanence of the parts of which he was constituted; that he might be, according to hypostasis, mediator to the parts of which he was composed, joining in himself the distance of the extremes, making peace, and reconciling human nature to the God and Father through the Spirit; [n: Ephes. ii, 16.] as being truly God according to essence, and having become truly man by nature according to economy; neither divided by the difference according to nature of his own parts, nor confused by the unity according to hypostasis of the same; but, by the principle of the community according to essence of the parts of which he was composed, being united according to nature to Father and Mother, he was shown preserving the difference toward one another of the parts of which he was composed; and, by the principle of the property according to hypostasis of his own parts, being distinguished from the extremes — Father, I mean, and Mother — he appeared having the singleness of his own hypostasis wholly without difference, unified always in the supreme personal identity of his own parts with one another.

⟦q20.1|2⟧ For the community according to essence of each of the parts with the extremes, in the unity of the one hypostasis, preserving unconfused the difference of the nature of each, did not allow the pious to recognize one nature of both from the union; lest there come about a total passing-away of the parts according to essence, or, by composition, the coming-to-be of one nature of the whole from them, having no way to guard the natural kinship of the extremes with the parts, not preserving the difference according to essence of the parts of which it was composed toward one another after the union; but it presented to be seen one hypostasis, composite from the union, maintained in the existence according to nature of the parts of which it was composed. ⟦q20.1|3⟧ And the property according to hypostasis of each of the parts, as toward the composition of some whole, Christ, being conceived together with the common according to essence, effected a common mark of the parts, that which characterizes the one hypostasis completed from them. ⟦q20.1|4⟧ For we say that the one hypostasis of Christ, completed from them by the concourse by natural union, that is true and real, has become common to flesh and Godhead in the ineffable union. ⟦q20.1|5⟧ And I say common, as the one and same most specific hypostasis of the parts, declared from the union; or rather, as being the one and same hypostasis of the Word, now and before: but before, without cause, simple and incomposite; and afterwards, for a cause, by assumption of flesh intellectually ensouled, become without change truly composite; according to which, marked off from the extremes — Father, I mean, and Mother — he is united to himself, having no difference whatsoever; lest the coming-to-be of a difference of the parts according to it become a total passing-away of the identity of the parts according to hypostasis, dissolving the union according to hypostasis into a personal dyad; [0557] having no way to show the personal identity of the parts toward one another preserved, being partitioned by the difference according to hypostasis into a dyad of persons.

⟦q20.2|1⟧ *That the things of the parts common according to hypostasis marked off the whole, that is Christ, from the extremes; but the things of the parts common according to essence with the extremes joined Christ, as a whole, to the extremes essentially.*

⟦q20.2|2⟧ For it was not by the idioms by which the flesh was marked off from the rest of men that it kept its difference from the Word; nor again would it be by the idioms by which it differed from the Word that it was also marked off from us. ⟦q20.2|3⟧ But by those by which it was marked off from us, by these it preserved its union according to hypostasis with the Word, that is its identity; and by those by which it was united to us naturally, by these, evidently, it had its essential difference from the Word preserved. ⟦q20.2|4⟧ Just as the Word too, by the idioms by which he was marked off from the common of the Godhead as Son and Word, by these preserved his union according to hypostasis with the flesh, that is his identity; and by those by which as God he kept the natural difference from the flesh, by these, being united according to essence to the Father and the Spirit, he interchanged the difference and the identity toward himself and the extremes: being united according to nature to the extremes by the communities of the parts, and distinguished according to hypostasis from the extremes by the properties of the same, namely the parts; in the identity according to hypostasis of his own parts showing the difference of the extremes toward himself preserved; and in the otherness according to essence of the parts bearing the natural identity of the extremes with the parts. ⟦q20.2|5+6⟧ And if he was united to the extremes by the communities of the parts, and marked off from the extremes by the properties of the parts, it is clear that by those by which he was united to the extremes he had the natural difference of the parts toward one another preserved; and by those by which he was distinguished from the extremes he had the identity according to hypostasis of the parts shining through.

⟦q20.2|7⟧ So then Christ had, of each of the parts of which he was composed, both the common and the specific: the common, in the principle of the identity according to essence of the extremes with his own parts, according to which he had the difference of the parts toward one another preserved even after the union; and the proper, in the principle of the identity according to hypostasis of the parts, according to which he was marked off from the extremes, preserving unconfused his distinction from them. ⟦q20.2|8⟧ For the true union according to hypostasis, of each of the parts coming together with one another into the composition of some whole, at their concourse at once toward the coming-to-be of some whole, taking along with the common the idiom that marks off each from the community according to essence, shows it enhypostatic, but not a hypostasis; because it did not subsist by itself separately, marked off from those of the same genus, or from that which subsists together with it by union toward the coming-to-be of some whole; which is proper to hypostasis. ⟦q20.2|9⟧ For hypostasis is that which subsists separately by itself; since they say that hypostasis is essence with idioms, differing in number from those of the same genus; and enhypostatic, that which by itself [0560] in no way subsists, but is contemplated in others, as a species in the individuals under it; or that which is composed with another, different according to essence, toward the coming-to-be of some whole; which, so far as it differs by the idioms that mark it off from those of the same genus according to essence, so far is it unified according to hypostasis with that with which it is composed, being identified with it. ⟦q20.2|10⟧ For it is not by the idioms by which it is marked off from those of the same genus that it is also distinguished from that with which it is composed by union and which subsists together with it; but by the marking-off idioms by which it differs from the consubstantial, by these it guards toward that with which it is composed the identity of the hypostasis by union, maintained in the wholly singular character of the person; which the true account upholds in the case of the divine economy, that is the incarnation. ⟦q20.2|11⟧ For by the idioms by which the flesh differed, marked off from us, by these it had its identity according to hypostasis with the Word; and by the idioms by which the Word differed from the Father and the Spirit, being marked off as Son, by these he kept that which is according to hypostasis toward the flesh singular and preserved, divided by no principle whatever.

⟦q20.2|12⟧ So then the flesh of God the Word is not a hypostasis. ⟦q20.2|13⟧ For never — not even for so long as the swiftness of a bare thought is by nature to pass — did it subsist at all by itself, separated from those of the same genus by the idioms that mark it off, or having its proper separated from the common toward the Word with whom it is composed according to hypostasis; but it is enhypostatic, as having received the coming-to-be of its being in him and through him; and having become his flesh by union, and being unified with him according to hypostasis by the principle of the property that marks it off from the rest of men. ⟦q20.2|14⟧ And, to say it more clearly, it makes its own the proper of the Word himself according to hypostasis; as it does also, it may be, the common according to essence, of which too it has truly become flesh by union.

⟦q20.3|1⟧ *That Christ, being perfect God, and the same perfect man, had the common and the proper of the things which he was, by which he made toward himself the union and distinction of the extremes: by the union of the extremes toward himself, assuring that the two natures of which he was composed are preserved in himself; and by the distinction of the extremes toward himself, presenting the singleness of his own hypostasis.*

⟦q20.3|2⟧ For if the Word, being God by nature, by assumption of flesh intellectually ensouled became man without change, it is clear that, being one from both, and through one assuring himself in truth to be both, the same had of both the common and the proper, by which he made the union and distinction toward the extremes: by the natural difference toward one another of his own parts being united according to essence to the extremes; and by the identity of the same, namely the parts, according to hypostasis, being distinguished from the extremes. ⟦q20.3|3+4⟧ And if the same had difference and identity, it is clear that the same was one and two: one, by the principle of his own undivided [0561] monad according to hypostasis; two, by the principle of the otherness according to essence of his own parts toward one another even after the union. ⟦q20.3|5+6⟧ For difference is a principle by which the otherness of the things signified toward one another is by nature preserved, and indicative of the how-it-is; and identity is an invariance by which the principle of the thing signified possesses the wholly singular, being recognized by no mode of difference.

⟦q20.4|1⟧ *A concise treatment of difference, by which it is shown that the quantity according to essence of the things united is preserved.*

⟦q20.4|2⟧ If, then, the difference according to essence toward one another of the parts of which he was composed is preserved in Christ after the union, there is a clear demonstration for the pious that the quantity according to essence of the things united has remained undiminished after the union, preserving the definition and principle of the essence of each part non-coinciding toward the other in the whole composed from them by composition. ⟦q20.4|3⟧ And if also the hypostatic identity of the whole toward its own parts — or rather, to speak more clearly, of the parts toward one another according to the composition of a whole from them — is preserved, it is clear that in no way do the parts, according to the one hypostasis completed from them, differ at all toward one another. ⟦q20.4|4⟧ And thus there is no account able to show either the difference according to essence being confused, or the singleness according to hypostasis being divided.

⟦q20.5|1⟧ *A broader and more natural demonstration concerning difference and quantity, and the number that indicates them.*

⟦q20.5|2⟧ So then, if we speak of difference in Christ after the union, and with every difference a quantity is by nature of necessity introduced together, of which we recognize number to be indicative, it is fitting that, taking up number for the sole indication of the difference of the things differing after the union, we in no way divide by the number the things signified, but indicate the existence of the things united as preserved; because every number is by nature indicative of quantity only, but not divisive. ⟦q20.5|3⟧ For that which neither acts nor is acted upon, how could it be master of any relation whatsoever? ⟦q20.5|4⟧ Since acting belongs to essence alone, as being acted upon belongs to accident, that is quality; from which, and out of which, those trained in such arguments say that the relations proceed. ⟦q20.5|5⟧ Or rather, as we examine it more exactly, not even of these — essence, I mean, and accident — are the relations the work, but of the cause and power that makes all things, and has placed in each of the things that have come to be a principle constitutive of its being, and presentative of its natural kinship and alienation toward one another. ⟦q20.5|6⟧ And if the relations properly do not have essence as their efficient cause, much less would they have number; which, insofar as it is not essence, cannot act at all; and insofar as it is not accident, that is quality, is not by nature acted upon. ⟦q20.5|7⟧ And if number is by nature neither to act nor to be acted upon, then it is a sign indicative of quantity, making no [0564] reflection of the relation in the quantity indicated; because it is not a genus able to be predicated of others, but a sign, as I said, reflective of the genus that is borne predicatively of others.

⟦q20.5|8⟧ And if number has neither acting nor being acted upon, it is clear that, insofar as it does not act, it is not indicative of any relation whatsoever, that is of union or division — for this, as I said, belongs to another principle — and insofar as it is not by nature acted upon, it is not divided. ⟦q20.5|9⟧ For in itself every number is undivided, not admitting movement by way of alteration, which is proper to quality; nor contraction and expansion, which is proper to essence; nor indeed increase and decrease, which is proper to quantity. ⟦q20.5|10⟧ And if it is indicative of quantity, but not of relation — for number is significative of quantity simply so called, but not of quantity called "in a certain way" — it is clear that number is by nature to make neither division nor indeed union, but only the indication of quantity, in whatever way we may conceive this to exist. ⟦q20.5|11⟧ For acting belongs, as I said, to essence, and being acted upon to accident, that is quality; of which number is neither. ⟦q20.5|12⟧ For if it were essence, it would also be self-subsistent, not needing another for its constitution; and if it were accident, that is quality, either it would make the difference most properly so called, and, combined with the genus, would assemble the definition of the subject, and would furnish the indication of a species, not of a quantity; or, conjoined with the species, it would introduce the difference commonly so called, and would make the distinction of one from another among the individuals brought under the same species; or it would make the difference properly so called in the aggregate of all the idioms in the individual; and it would show what is signified through it to be not only other from other, but also of another kind. ⟦q20.5|13⟧ And if nobody at all takes in number along with the definitions of things, number is neither essence nor quality. ⟦q20.5|14⟧ And if number is none of these, it is clear that it is by nature neither to act nor to be acted upon; and if number has not acting and being acted upon, it fittingly neither unites at all, nor divides as acting; nor indeed is it divided at all as being acted upon; but, making the indication of the quantity of things alone, it does not touch their relation.

⟦q20.5|15⟧ And if number is indicative of quantity alone, but not of any relation whatsoever conceived in respect of it, number is rather a word, so to speak, and a name yoked to a quantity. ⟦q20.5|16⟧ So then he who speaks of difference in Christ after the union has also wholly conceived, together with the difference, a quantity of the things differing. ⟦q20.5|17⟧ For what is wholly simple is also not receptive of difference; because it is also in every way the same with itself and singular, and whole, and wholly without relation, as not having the genus in quantity predicated of it; and for this reason it is wholly not numbered. ⟦q20.5|17⟧ But he who has conceived a quantity together with the difference

⟦q21.1|1⟧ [0565] — for it is impossible that a difference be known that is wholly without share in quantity — taking up number for the sole indication, but not the division, of the quantity conceived together with the difference, does not depart from the truth, signifying through number the quantity conceived of necessity with the difference; since, as I said, with every difference spoken of in whatever mode a quantity is wholly conceived together, of which we have been taught that number is by nature indicative, but not divisive. ⟦q21.1|2⟧ And if he who signifies through number the quantity conceived of necessity together with the difference, for the establishing of the mystery, cares rather for the truth, that he may not uphold a confusion of the things united: how must one not think it just, according to some, that for this cause the quantity according to essence of the things preserved without confusion in Christ after the union be confessed, since number, as the argument has shown at length, is by nature to make indication of difference, but not of division?

⟦q21.1|3⟧ If, then, the principle according to essence of each of the things united remained unaltered after the union, not falling over into the definition of the essence of the other, manifestly the Godhead of Christ has remained according to essence Godhead, not migrating into the essence of flesh; and again the manhood of the same has remained according to essence manhood, not suffering change into the nature of the Godhead. ⟦q21.1|4⟧ And if neither the Godhead of Christ nor the manhood have migrated into each other on account of the union according to hypostasis, but the natures have remained unconfused, according to the great Cyril, clearly the difference after the union is of the natures preserved in Christ, and no account will gainsay it. ⟦q21.1|5+6+7+8+9+10⟧ And if the difference after the union is of the things preserved without confusion in Christ, and it is impossible that a difference be known wholly separated from quantity, let the quantity of the things differing be spoken of for the indication of the difference, that we may not be suspected of upholding a difference somehow bare and not true. ⟦q21.1|5+6+7+8+9+10⟧ For the account knows every difference to be bare that does not have the truth of the things whose difference it is existentially underlying it.

⟦q21.1|5+6+7+8+9+10⟧ We, then, never saying properly that hypostasis and nature are the same, according to the teaching of the Fathers, as the argument demonstrated above beforehand, piously uphold in Christ the identity and the difference: the one, by the principle of the one hypostasis, according to which we confess God the Word the same as his own flesh, lest the all-praised Trinity receive a personal addition, becoming a tetrad; the other, by the principle of the otherness according to essence of the things from which he is, according to which the parts of Christ in no way fall over into one another. ⟦q21.1|5+6+7+8+9+10⟧ For Godhead and manhood could never become the same according to essence, that nothing created may be, by union, connatural and consubstantial with the Godhead. ⟦q21.1|5+6+7+8+9+10⟧ Since to say that nature is consubstantial with nature we know to belong to a raving understanding. ⟦q21.1|11⟧ For [0568] nature could never be connatural and consubstantial with nature. ⟦q21.1|12⟧ For the connatural and consubstantial is by nature said only of the individuals of the same species referred to one essence.

⟦q21.2|1⟧ *That he who does not say nature and hypostasis are the same piously speaks of the union and the difference in Christ, believing the one to be according to hypostasis, the other according to nature.*

⟦q21.2|2⟧ Holding this confession, we neither deny the difference of the natures after the union, believing that the natures from which the one Christ was composed have remained unconfused; nor are we ignorant of the union according to hypostasis, confessing Christ one of the holy and consubstantial and worshipful Trinity. ⟦q21.2|3⟧ But we also make manifest through number the difference of the things from which Christ is, preserved in him in truth without cut and confusion after the union, for the destruction of the confusion of Apollinarius and Eutyches; and, since we know the union according to hypostasis, with a piercing voice we put forward the one nature of God the Word incarnate, by flesh having the intellectual soul, against the division of Nestorius; fleeing equally both the confusion of Apollinarius, who denies the natural difference of the things united after the union, and upholds that the flesh is the same as the Word according to nature on account of the union; and the division of Nestorius, who proclaims a hypostatic difference in Christ, and cuts asunder the identity according to hypostasis of the flesh with the Word.

⟦q21.3|1⟧ *That Severus, saying nature and hypostasis are the same, makes the union confusion and the difference division; by which he is shown both distending the Word of the Trinity into a tetrad of persons, and cutting the mystery of the Monad into a dyad of Godheads, and blasphemously casting Christ out of all essential existence.*

⟦q21.3|2⟧ But Severus, saying essence and hypostasis, and nature and person, properly the same in the matter of the divine incarnation, neither knows the union as unconfused, even if he pretends to say so; nor the difference as undivided, even if he goes through this too boastfully; but he works the union into confusion after Apollinarius, and the difference into alienation after Nestorius. ⟦q21.3|3⟧ For if hypostasis and nature are the same, in no way, according to him, will the flesh be the same as the Word or other. ⟦q21.3|4⟧ For, saying the difference in Christ after the union as in a natural quality, he too will be found dividing the union along with Nestorius, introducing the difference according to hypostasis of the flesh toward the Word after the union; since according to him the difference as in a natural quality cannot be conceived otherwise than as difference in a hypostatic quality, if indeed nature and hypostasis are the same. ⟦q21.3|5⟧ For, saying the difference as in a natural quality, he is compelled to bring in besides the difference as in a hypostatic quality too, that he may show the hypostasis being the same as the [0569] nature. ⟦q21.3|5⟧ And if he does not speak of the difference according to hypostasis after the union, fearing the division, let him not speak of the natural difference after the union either, if indeed he has resolved to be consistent with himself, and is eager to keep the canons he himself defined for himself; since he knows nature and hypostasis to be the same as each other in definition and principle.

⟦q21.3|6⟧ And if according to both — I mean the nature and the hypostasis — the flesh has no difference toward the Word, as Severus thinks, let the same flesh clearly be both consubstantial and of one hypostasis with the same, according to the drivel of Apollinarius. ⟦q21.3|7⟧ And if the flesh is consubstantial with the Word, it will be consubstantial with the Father and the Spirit too, and the Trinity will appear become a tetrad; since things consubstantial do not admit concurrence according to hypostasis with one another. ⟦q21.3|8⟧ And if the flesh is of one hypostasis with the Word, it will be of other essence toward him. ⟦q21.3|9⟧ For things of one hypostasis have the principle of essence toward one another in every way and wholly different. ⟦q21.3|10⟧ And if the flesh, as being of one hypostasis with the Word after the union, is of other essence toward him, Christ will be found cut into two natures, according to Severus, after the union; if indeed, according to him, number wholly possesses the power of division. ⟦q21.3|11⟧ Thus everything that fights against the truth is easily turned round upon itself and easily trips over itself.

⟦q21.3|12⟧ And if, saying one composite nature of Christ after the union, he thinks to attach to it the difference as in a natural quality: first, insofar as it is a composite nature, Christ will be consubstantial with nothing at all of the things that are; if indeed Christ is one and alone according to essence and nature. ⟦q21.3|13⟧ For nature, as has been said, could never at all be consubstantial with nature. ⟦q21.3|14⟧ And not being consubstantial with any of the things that are, the same will be neither God at all, nor indeed man; or, if God, he who upholds this will be a polytheist, saying the Father and the Holy Spirit God of an incomposite nature, as simple, and saying Christ God of a composite nature, as not simple; and he will be shown proclaiming two Godheads, one simple and one composite.

⟦q21.3|14⟧ Then, saying the difference as in a natural quality: if he says that the natures whose qualities they are underlie the different qualities, he too will be shown making indication of two natures after the union, becoming, even unwilling, the advocate of those against whom he contended. ⟦q21.3|15⟧ But if he says that the difference is of bare qualities apart from the things, let him say that the union too is of these. ⟦q21.3|16⟧ For of whatever the difference is after the union, of these manifestly is the union; and in a few syllables he has brought in the spontaneity of Epicurus and the deceitful [0572] phantasy of Mani, Christ not existing for him in truth in the things, but in empty qualities; and Christ will be God by quality alone, and not in reality. ⟦q21.3|17⟧ For immortality and mortality, of which he says the difference after the union is, belong to natures, but are not natures. ⟦q21.3|18⟧ For where is mortality, if that which dies does not exist; or immortality, if the nature which death does not touch does not exist? ⟦q21.3|19⟧ But truly wickedness is a thing unreasoning, and seeming to be wise is by nature the craftsman of great folly. ⟦q21.3|20⟧ And for the prudent it is more profitable to embrace silence toward such men than to be thought, by refuting them, themselves too to make sport of the mystery. ⟦q21.3|21⟧ Severus, then, holding such views about Christ, has died together with his own doctrines.

⟦q21.4|1⟧ *A concise exposition, with the right advocacy of the true confession of the Fathers.*

⟦q21.4|2⟧ But we do not think thus, we do not believe thus, *nor is this the portion of Jacob*, says he who said it; but, confessing that the one Christ, that is his one hypostasis, was composed from two natures that are perfect according to the proper principle of their being — Godhead, I mean, and manhood — we believe the natures to have remained unconfused, and without any cut whatsoever, after the union. ⟦q21.4|3⟧ For, believing Christ to be, we have confessed that the natures from which he was composed are preserved after the union. ⟦q21.4|4⟧ And for this reason we proclaim in him a natural difference, looking, namely, at the parts from which he was composed, because Godhead and manhood are not the same according to essence; but a hypostatic identity; for the flesh is manifestly the same as the Word according to hypostasis. ⟦q21.4|5⟧ Knowing, then, a real difference after the union of the natures from which Christ was composed, we are not ignorant of the identity according to hypostasis; since the parts of Christ, while differing naturally from one another, do not differ according to the hypostasis of the whole composed from them; because the completive parts of the whole are by nature not to differ from one another at all by the principle of the whole.

⟦q21.4|6⟧ Confessing, then, the same to be two natures, we have recognized the same to be at once God and man; if indeed we do not put forward the names in his case as bare and lying upon nothing. ⟦q21.4|7⟧ And believing him to be in two natures undividedly and unconfusedly after the union, we have proclaimed the same perfect in Godhead and the same perfect in manhood, as a whole in parts. ⟦q21.4|8⟧ For in the parts from which he was composed, in them, as a whole, he is recognized after [0573] the union; because Christ has no other indication of his being and being preserved and being properly spoken of than the natural abiding in being, after the union, of the parts of which he was composed. ⟦q21.4|9⟧ For not only from these, but also in these, and, to speak more properly, these is Christ. ⟦q21.4|9⟧ And we number after the union the things from which Christ is, not partitioning — God forbid — the natures, but making only the indication of their difference preserved after the union.

⟦q21.4|10⟧ And we confess a concourse by natural union, that is true and real, according to the holy Cyril, and we uphold the all-holy Virgin as Theotokos properly and in truth; not simply, but as having truly conceived God the Word himself, begotten ineffably of God and Father before all the ages, and having given birth to him, incarnate ineffably from her. ⟦q21.4|11⟧ And we say that both the miracles and the passions are of the same, Christ being evidently one, who operates the divine things and the human: the divine in a fleshly way, because through flesh not without share in natural operation he put forth the power of the miracles; and the human in a divine way, because without natural compulsion, by authority, willing it, he accepted the experience of the human passions. ⟦q21.4|12+13⟧ Of the same we say the cross and the death and the burial and the resurrection and the ascension into the heavens; whence he came down without flesh, not passing over by changing places, he who in no way is contained by the things that are; for he is free of all circumscription; but he appeared through flesh, as by nature loving man, becoming, by a true birth from a woman, containable among our things, as he willed. ⟦q21.4|14⟧ Wherefore we bring to him, incarnate too, one worship with the Father and the Spirit.

⟦q21.4|15⟧ If, then, with soul and mouth we call the holy Virgin properly Theotokos, and confess the union according to hypostasis, and say that a natural concourse by union has come about, and uphold one Lord and Christ and Son, and one nature of God the Word himself incarnate; and believe Christ to be one of the holy Trinity, worshipped together and numbered together with the Father and the Spirit: how are we reviled as dividing by the fearless and by those who dare to say all things falsely, on account only of the bare word of number, put forward by us, as has often been said, for the sole indication of the difference of the natures preserved after the union? ⟦q21.4|16⟧ If, then, number of necessity wholly makes a division of the quantity of things, let someone show this, and we accept the calumny as true. ⟦q21.4|17⟧ But if they only abuse, slandering those who proclaim the true faith of the mystery, on account of the love of glory with which they are sick, they themselves will give account to God in the day of judgement, says the divine Apostle, [n: Rom. xiv, 12.] on behalf of those whom they have swept away into error — those, that is, who have completed the long course of the present life. ⟦q21.4|18⟧ But those [0576] who survive, may they have God changing them, and leading them to the knowledge of the truth now warred against by them. ⟦q21.4|19⟧ For so it is fitting to speak, on account of the commandment that bids us pray for those who curse. [n: Matth. v, 44; Luc. vi, 28.]

⟦q21.4|20⟧ I, then, have gone through these things for thee in epitome, concerning what thou didst ask, servant of God, for the sake of thy full assurance; not holding one thing in soul, as some of those who rejoice in Severus noise abroad, and speaking another to those I happen upon — do not think this — but as I was taught, so I both think and believe, and what I received from the Fathers, I speak. ⟦q21.4|21⟧ And, to say it more properly, I put forward my very understanding embodied in words. ⟦q21.4|22⟧ And if my word is false, may I not attain the blessedness there; but may I carry off, as the fruit of falsehood, alienation from the good things promised. ⟦q21.4|23⟧ And do thou, for the sake of my ready obedience, deign to repay me with thy prayers; that Christ our God, the salvation of those who fear him, may heal the wounds of my soul by the power of the mystery of his life-giving sufferings for us; who alone with the Father and the Holy Spirit is glorified unto the ages. ⟦q21.4|24⟧ Amen.

⟦q21.5|1⟧ SCHOLIA

⟦q21.6|1⟧ 1. ⟦q21.6|2⟧ In the definition, he says, the community of the things the same according to essence is contemplated.

⟦q21.7|1⟧ 2. ⟦q21.7|2⟧ That the idioms characterizing the composite hypostasis, he says, are common to its parts.

⟦q21.8|1⟧ 3. ⟦q21.8|2⟧ Exact definition of hypostasis.

⟦q21.9|1⟧ 4. ⟦q21.9|2⟧ Definition, that is description, of the enhypostatic.

⟦q21.10|1⟧ 5. ⟦q21.10|2⟧ Generic definition of difference.

⟦q21.11|1⟧ 6. ⟦q21.11|2⟧ Generic definition of identity.

⟦q21.12|1⟧ 7. ⟦q21.12|2⟧ Quantity is called a genus, of which number is indicative.

⟦q21.13|1⟧ 8. ⟦q21.13|2⟧ He said "properly", because their own definitions do not change into one another. ⟦q21.13|3⟧ For of things of which the definition is not one, the principle is not one; and of things of which the principle is not one, the mode of being is different; and of things of which the mode of being is different, the identity through all is not. ⟦q21.13|4⟧ So then nature and hypostasis are not the same; because the mode of being and principle and definition of these is not properly one. ⟦q21.13|5⟧ So it will be called hypostasis by misuse, and perhaps relatively, but not properly.

⟦q22.1|1⟧ LETTER 16

⟦q22.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q22.1|2⟧ When I was being tormented with many scourges of grief over what had befallen, through the calumny of those who do not fear the Lord, the noble guardian of the virtues, the lord [0577] George, thy holy letter, arriving in good time, consoled me, holy Father: on the one hand bringing good tidings of thy health, longed for by all; on the other making known the firm footing of thy right confession in the Lord Jesus Christ, our true God. ⟦q22.1|3⟧ This, confirmed by the testing of sufferings, has been transformed for thee into unchangeableness, since thou hast set at nought the misfortune of the present life, whose prosperity thou didst long ago hold easy to despise, and hast moored the whole longing of the soul toward the things hoped for alone; a longing which faith and a good conscience are wont to characterize: the one guarding the pious word, stained in no way by the falsehood of opinion; the other fashioning the life pleasing to God, brought to success by the keeping of the commandments. ⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ For of both — of word, I mean, and of life — there is need for him who has been zealous to prefer above all things the life lived together with wisdom; so that we may also piously proclaim the mystery of the unchangeable self-emptying, for our sake, through flesh, of the God of all things; confessing Christ in truth God and man together: the one being without cause, through himself; the other having become, later, for us. ⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ Neither putting off what he was because of what he became — for he is unchangeable; nor denying what he became because of what he was — for he loves man. ⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ For to him alone, who properly is, it was possible by an abundance of power to become, without change, without confusion, what he was not, and to remain properly both, what he was and what he became; at the extreme of his own hypostatic monad, which in its singleness admits no division, on account of the difference, naturally preserved in him after the union, of the things from which he was composed; since, united according to hypostasis to flesh intellectually ensouled, by the miracles he was shown remaining without change what he was, and by the sufferings he appeared preserving without alteration what he had become; and through both — the miracles, I mean, and the sufferings — he bestowed on us both the abolition of sin and the grace of deification.

⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ And let us honour the calling of grace by a conduct of life made splendid by the commandments, shaken in no respect by any pleasure or by pain of whatever kind; and let us show, by what we do or suffer, that we cling to God alone, who gave himself for us as a ransom and an exchange, [n: I Tim. ii, 6.] having entrusted to him our whole life, the present and the future: as being from him by the principle of our bringing-into-being, and existing through him by the mode of our conduct, and coming to our end in him by the mystery, according to grace, of the deification hoped for. ⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ And let us receive the ever-well-being through what now seems an "alas"-being, having shown, through what we suffer, our disposition toward God that lies hidden, altered in no way by the painful things that befall us.

⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ [0580] Let us not then lose heart in afflictions, precious Father, [n: Ephes. iii, 13.] knowing that affliction works patience, and patience proof, and proof hope, and hope does not put to shame, [n: Rom. v, 3-5.] having secure the enjoyment of the everlasting goods for which we suffer; but let us bear nobly what befalls us, looking to the author of our salvation, Jesus, [n: Hebr. ii, 10.] who, being true God, accepted, willing it, the death for our sake; so that, suffering, we may rejoice, having become planted together with him through death, [n: Rom. vi, 5.] leavened toward the resurrection of indestructible life. ⟦q22.1|4+5+6+7+8+9⟧ Since it is the wish of the divine law that we, doing good, should suffer unjustly, as disciples of Christ, for the condemnation of sin — the sin which the intelligible serpent brought in, having by deceit persuaded man to transgress the divine commandment and to prefer the temporal to the everlasting; whom we have received authority to tread upon, [n: Luc. x, 19.] guarding the guarding grace, which is kept safe by the keeping of the commandments; which keeping is achieved by those who love the Lord only by hardship of the flesh, voluntary and involuntary, with sober reasoning. ⟦q22.1|10⟧ Having nailed all thy desire to him, thou dost not feel the afflictions, holy Father, since thou hast the joy of the things hoped for overcoming the present affliction. ⟦q22.1|10⟧ May we all attain them, Christ our great God and Saviour [n: Tit. ii, 13.] distributing these things to us, as he knows, as each is worthy; by the intercession of his immaculate and all-holy Mother, ever-virgin, and of all the saints. ⟦q22.1|11⟧ Amen.

⟦q22.2|1⟧ LETTER 17

⟦q22.2|1⟧ *Of the same, to Julian the Scholasticus of Alexandria, on the Church's doctrine concerning the incarnation of the Lord.*

⟦q22.2|2⟧ With great joy and spiritual gladness the precious letter of my God-guarded master filled me, the lowly and sinful one, and with me, so to say, the whole holy catholic Church of God; bringing us good tidings of the firm and immovable inclination — his own and that of my master who is with him, the lord Christopemptus, the most wise scholasticus — concerning the right and pious and saving confession of the faith in Christ our true God; who became man for this reason: that he might gather the nature of men to himself, and bring it to a stand from being borne badly toward itself — or rather, from being at faction against itself and divided, and having no rest, because of the unsteady movement of the inclination in each one. ⟦q22.2|3⟧ Therefore, all of us giving thanks to God for you, most precious ones, make petitions without ceasing, sinners though we are, that you may preserve, unshaken and [0581] unmoved for all time, the saving confession of the faith; believing that the Son and Word of God and Father, having truly become man from men, for men, after the manner of men, without sin, was not changed from being God even in becoming man; nor indeed was he diminished in being truly man, having remained what he was and is and ever will be, God according to nature; but, being God according to essence and nature through himself, he truly became man without change, by way of economy, for us — by the assumption, that is, of flesh having an intellectual and rational soul. ⟦q22.2|4⟧ Therefore the same is God by nature and truly, and the same is man by nature and truly, as being, the same, in truth this and that without deficiency; inasmuch as none of the natures brought together into the union according to hypostasis in the mystery of his incarnation, that is, of his perfect becoming man, has in any way denied itself because of the union, but each keeps its own essential principle and definition — that is, its natural property — unconfused and untransmuted with respect to the other after the union. ⟦q22.2|5⟧ For the union does not abolish the difference of the things brought together into one hypostasis; rather, it drives out altogether their division part from part.

⟦q22.2|6⟧ Since, then, the things from which Christ is are preserved in Christ, undiminished according to nature, even after the union, how is it not just that the things from which Christ is should be confessed even after the union — as some hold, not seeing together how perilous it is and dangerous, and bearing every harm to the soul, to fight against what is plain, and to deny the truth that manifestly is; and that this is proper to those alone who do not expect resurrection and judgement at all?
