⟦q22.2|7⟧ For to whom is the word of the truth not manifest and evident, as simple and unelaborate, and having nothing riddling at all? ⟦q22.2|8⟧ For if Christ is God and man in fact and in truth after the union, and is not said to be God and man only in name and appellation, it is plain that he who says and thinks Christ to be God and man together after the union has confessed, along with the names, that the natures whose names they are truly are — if indeed he does not think that the appellations are applied to Christ bare and altogether empty of realities. ⟦q22.2|9⟧ What absurdity, then, has the holy catholic Church of God committed, if it says that the things from which Christ is are preserved in Christ according to nature, by an unsundered union, without confusion and without division, even after the union? ⟦q22.2|10⟧ And what has it thought that is alien to the teaching of the holy Fathers, if it holds neither that the divine nature of the Word has been changed into the nature of the flesh, nor that the nature of the flesh itself has passed over into the nature of the Word because of the union, and teaches the faithful to think and to say so after the union? ⟦q22.2|11⟧ And what of the Fathers' tradition does it not rather confirm, teaching to think thus, and confessing one Lord and one Christ, and one Son, the same, and one nature of God the [0584] Word himself, who was incarnate — a nature incarnate in flesh having the rational and intellectual soul; taking the expression according to the interpretation of our holy and blessed Father Cyril, understanding that by saying "incarnate" the signification of our essence is brought in, and not denying the nature of the Word's flesh because of the union? ⟦q22.2|12⟧ For the things preserved according to nature, without confusion and without change and without division, by an unsundered union in Christ, even after the union, without any diminution whatever — it is altogether most fitting and most just that they should also be confessed after the union. ⟦q22.2|13⟧ For it is proper to true faith to confess what we have been taught to think. ⟦q22.2|14⟧ If, then, the things from which Christ is are preserved according to nature after the union, it is also altogether just to think that they are in Christ after the union. ⟦q22.2|15⟧ And if it is just to think that the things from which Christ is are preserved after the union, much more would it be at once most just and most fitting, and most urgent for all who take thought for salvation, to confess them; if indeed the proof of true faith is the confession of it that falls short in nothing. ⟦q22.2|16⟧ So much on this. ⟦q22.2|17+18+19⟧ For there is no need to say more, when one addresses the word to you, and to men who through grace are ignorant of nothing of what bears on the right faith of Christ.

⟦q22.2|17+18+19⟧ Concerning what you have written to me, most precious of all to me, I referred it to the blessed servant of God, the all-praised prefect, giving him also your precious syllables; and he promised readily to fulfil your every bidding. ⟦q22.2|17+18+19⟧ And I do not cease reminding him, until he bring your bidding fully to its end. ⟦q22.2|20⟧ And may the Lord and God of all things and Saviour Jesus Christ, who accepted willingly the death for us, and redeemed us by his own blood from the power of darkness, [n: Coloss. i, 15.] and gave us the commandment new, in our loving one another as much as we have been loved [n: Joan. xiii, 34.] — may he himself hold your right hand, [n: Psal. lxxii, 24.] and guide you into every good work, and direct your steps that are according to him; bringing you in the fullness of the fruits of righteousness into the wonderful place of his tabernacle, where is the dwelling of all who rejoice. [n: Psal. lxxxvi, 7.]

⟦q23.1|1+2⟧ LETTER 18

⟦q23.1|1+2⟧ *Of the same, in the person of George, the all-praised prefect of Africa, to the nuns who had departed from the catholic Church in Alexandria.*

⟦q23.1|1+2⟧ I, indeed, thought that you had truly been well established, and had the footings of the soul immovable from the right and pious confession and hope that accord with the blameless and unstumbling faith of Christ, reverencing the grace of God that called you and united you to the living and sound and blameless [0585] body of the holy catholic and apostolic Church; the grace that strengthened you when you were already wasting away with the consumptive sickness of error and ignorance, and made you a serviceable member of the common body of the Church; in which the pious and right and true and saving word of the apostolic faith is preached in its flower, bringing within itself the whole region under heaven, and ever bringing and adding what is lacking to what has already been gathered, and showing that all, from the ends of the earth to its ends, have one soul and one tongue in the spirit, according to the concord and unison of the faith; the faith which the Word of God, the Creator of men, having become man ineffably from men, for men, after the manner of men, truly, by the assumption of flesh having a rational and intellectual soul, and not by a change of the Godhead — without sin, that is — taught us men to confess and to embrace: denying none at all of his natures after the union, that is, after the becoming man; neither the divine, according to which he both is and abides ever God, through himself, and consubstantial with the Father; nor the human, according to which the same truly became man for us, as loving man, and is consubstantial with us. ⟦q23.1|3+4⟧ For neither was he changed from what he was and is and will be for ever, God according to nature; nor indeed did he transmute the nature of the ensouled flesh — of what he became, man, by an unsundered union — being one and the same, God and man: not being God and man in name and appellation only, but in fact and in truth both being properly God and man, and truly so named; having the one and singular from the union according to hypostasis, but not according to nature, as the Fathers teach, saying: «For even if the both-together is one, yet not by nature, by the concourse»; by which concourse, by way of economy, for us, he, as good, consenting to become composite, keeps unimpaired, even after the incarnation, the simplicity according to nature by which he is of one nature with the Father and abides consubstantial. ⟦q23.1|5+6+7⟧ For Godhead and manhood are not the same. ⟦q23.1|5+6+7⟧ For even if the Word of God has become man, yet it is by the assumption of flesh intellectually [?] united to him according to hypostasis without sundering that he has become man. ⟦q23.1|5+6+7⟧ Therefore the same is properly God and man, as being that and having become this; and being this and that without deficiency: neither confused by the extreme singleness of the hypostasis, according to which the principle of the difference is altogether absent; nor divided by the pure otherness of the natures of which he is constituted, according to which the principle of the difference has in no way been abolished because of the union.

⟦q23.1|5+6+7⟧ Since, then, the difference of the natures from which Christ is is preserved without confusion even after the union, there is, I think, no account at all able to show that it is just that the quantity of the things that differ naturally and are preserved in Christ by an unsundered union [0588] should not be confessed; because every difference brings in with itself, altogether, a quantity of certain things that differ from one another in some respect. ⟦q23.1|8+9⟧ For no one, however resourceful he be and most inventive, will ever be able to say that there is difference without quantity, if he thinks rightly and takes even a little account of truth; knowing clearly that every difference is either of the otherness of certain things according to essence, or of the unlikeness of certain things according to quality and property; a difference which the quantity of the subject realities, whether they are in union or apart from it, altogether produces. ⟦q23.1|10⟧ For in things in which one and the same principle is not contemplated in every way, there is a reflection of quantity, to which difference is proper. ⟦q23.1|11⟧ If, then, in Christ it follows, by reason, upon the union to speak of the singleness in every way according to hypostasis, as the Fathers teach; and it is consequent and fitting to speak of the quantity with the difference of the natures from which Christ is, preserved in Christ without confusion and without division even after the union: it is plain that, just as he who does not say that Christ, because of the hypostatic union, is one incarnate nature of God the Word — rightly understood according to the interpretation of our most holy Father and teacher Cyril — does not believe that the union has come about; so also he who does not confess the two natures after the union, from which Christ is, cannot say that the difference is preserved. ⟦q23.1|12⟧ For the difference after the union is of the natural otherness of the things from which Christ is, and not of an unlikeness according to quality alone, apart from the natures subject to the qualities — without which it is among the impossibilities that quality should exist by itself; lest we bring in a difference that is bare, or rather, to speak more properly, false and without existence.

⟦q23.1|13⟧ Persuaded that you held this confession of the faith exactly according to the Fathers, I did not shrink from writing, time and again, to all everywhere — to patriarchs and bishops and rulers, and to our most pious and all-gentle emperors themselves — for thanksgiving to God, making manifest to all your genuine conversion to Christ, and your faith. ⟦q23.1|14⟧ And I never thought, as I said, that you would so quickly be removed from your saving calling. ⟦q23.1|15⟧ But since, because of my sins, the evil one, who from the beginning has been dragging the race of men down by deceit into ruin and destruction, has prevailed to persuade you too to prefer the lie to the truth, and, for the sake of an evil habit, to gratify, more than God and the goods laid up for the saints, deceiving men who have been taught only to bait toward death; and has been able to turn you back, like dogs — to speak with the prophet — to your own vomit: [n: Prov. xxvi, 11.] I charge you, if indeed you are so incurably disposed, and have resolved to turn away from the grace of Christ to the end, for the present to give the [0589] gifts made to you by me to Theopemptus, my man, sent by me for this alone, with a written order, so that you may have no doubt about this. ⟦q23.1|16⟧ And again, whatever seems good to our most pious and Christ-loving and all-gentle emperors and to the most holy patriarchs, when they learn all your affairs through me — who, with God, am to be in the presence of their serenity — concerning your apostasy and so great wickedness and ingratitude, I shall judge it well that this be done. ⟦q23.1|17⟧ For I will not pass over in silence so great an evil. ⟦q23.1|18⟧ But if, taking to mind the dread tribunal of Christ, before which, in the presence of angels and archangels and of the whole people of the hosts of heaven, and of all human nature, my suit against you on this matter will be brought, you repent and return to God, who calls you again in grace, then I wish you to have the very foundation already given you by me; and I shall not cease, as long as I am granted, by way of economy, to live this present and temporal life, devising ways toward your more abundant support; and I would not refuse to give myself as a ransom for your souls, if occasion should of necessity call for this to be done. ⟦q23.1|19⟧ And I will give it readily, God calling me to this through grace; if only you restore yourselves again to Christ God, and by your return heal well the cut and the separation from his body that has befallen you.

⟦q23.2|1⟧ LETTER 19

⟦q23.2|1⟧ *Of the same, to Pyrrhus, the most holy presbyter and abbot.*

⟦q23.2|2+3+4+5+6+7⟧ Having read thy holy letter, God-honoured Father, I recognized thy mind as wholly imbued by the whole Spirit; and I discerned it to have gone out from natural intelligence by as much as I perceived it to have laid hold of the divine wisdom that is above nature. ⟦q23.2|2+3+4+5+6+7⟧ For I seemed, as it were, to see thy God-wise mind itself present through the letter, flashing with the pure rays of the Spirit alone, by which he who is counted worthy to be [0592] adopted as a son by the Father of all is wont to be transformed toward the more divine; and redeeming me from ignorance of the divine doctrines by as much as I had formerly seemed to hold fast to knowledge of them. ⟦q23.2|2+3+4+5+6+7⟧ Therefore, marvelling at thy sagacity in all things, I cried out and uttered only that word which of old a woman cried to the Lord, struck by the power of his words: Blessed is the womb that bore thee, and the breasts that thou didst suck. [n: Luc. xi, 27.] And I call blessed not only her who bore thee, precious Father, as counted worthy to become the mother of such a one, but also thy understanding, which ever conceives, and is pregnant with, and gives birth to the pious word, as having been shown by grace a womb able to contain the Word who is above nature, through the appropriation of thy inclination to the Word himself; and indeed thy firm habits as well, issued from thy heart, by which, like breasts, thou nursest the Word in practice and in contemplation, as he grows along with the supply of pious concepts and manners, and — to speak paradoxically — makes his own growth the deification of the mind that nurses him. ⟦q23.2|2+3+4+5+6+7⟧ For what else had I to utter to thee, precious Father, beholding thee become the begetter of such words? ⟦q23.2|2+3+4+5+6+7⟧ Through which, as from a mountain — the height of knowledge — thou hast made known the decree on the divine doctrines, brought and issued like God-engraved tablets through the new Mediator and great Moses of our day, the priest of priests and head of the divine priesthood throughout the whole inhabited world; which the holy and alone-worshipped Trinity dictated as through an instrument, the said high priest, and by which it bound the Churches more firmly to concord, so that they set at nought the innovation concerning the faith that seemed to the many to have taken place in the city of Alexander.

⟦q23.2|8⟧ For the decree that was given preserved entire that conception according to which our God-bearing Fathers handed down to the holy Churches to believe, saying that God the Word is one and the same both before the flesh and with the flesh; the flesh which he himself, for us, united to himself according to hypostasis, intellectually ensouled, assumed from the holy Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary; himself deigning to become the seed of his own incarnation, that he might also truly become man, and show, by the seedless conception and the incorrupt birth, nature innovated, and suffering no diminution by the innovation. ⟦q23.2|9+10⟧ For the mystery was God's self-chosen self-emptying, out of goodness toward men, and not a falling away from Godhead — the voluntary condescension through flesh. ⟦q23.2|9+10⟧ For he remained what he was, even becoming what he was not — for he is unchangeable; and he kept what he became, abiding what he was — for he loves man. ⟦q23.2|11⟧ Through what he operated in a manner befitting God, showing what he became unaltered; and [0593] through what he suffered in a manner befitting man, giving assurance that what he was was not changed. ⟦q23.2|12⟧ For he operated the divine things fleshly, since through flesh not without a share of natural operation; and the human things divinely, since it was by volition, with authority, and not by force of circumstance, that he admitted the experience of human sufferings. ⟦q23.2|13⟧ For neither the divine things divinely, since he was not God naked; nor the human things fleshly, since he was not mere man. ⟦q23.2|14⟧ Therefore the miracles were not without passion, and the sufferings were not without miracle; but the former — if I may speak boldly — not impassible, the latter manifestly wondrous; and both paradoxical, because both divine [and human], as proceeding from one and the same God the Word incarnate, who through both gave real assurance of the truth of the things from which, and which, he was. ⟦q23.2|15⟧ For what is brought to completion from certain things without confusion, by the natural union that is by concourse, both keeps unchanged the natures from which it is constituted, and preserves undiminished their constitutive powers, for the fulfilment of one work, whether what came to pass was passion or miracle; after the image of thy wonderful example, Father, so apt to the mystery of the divine incarnation: the sword heated in the fire, whose cut we know to be burning, and whose burning we know to be cutting. ⟦q23.2|16⟧ For a concourse of fire and iron has come about according to hypostasis, neither having departed from its power according to nature because of the union with the other; nor indeed possessing it at large after the union, and separated from that with which it is compounded and co-subsists.

⟦q23.2|17⟧ This, then, is the exactness of the divine doctrines that I found wisely woven into your letters; upon which, according to the moderate power of thinking that is in me, I fixed my mind, and prayed to be made sufficient to guard it and to hold it fast in my understanding, admitting no erasure from any forgetting. ⟦q23.2|18⟧ But thou, God-honoured Father, have sympathy with me thy servant, and, as one who loves man, grant the pardon due to one who is unable to undertake what has been commanded. ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ And do not condemn me of disobedience, but rather approve me as one eager to become aware of his own ignorance. ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ For rashness is hateful, and the first offspring of the uttermost ignorance; in which the disease of pride is wont to show itself through, fashioning in those it has seized a habit opposed to God. ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ And accept my excuse in this as pure of every passion; and do not thrust me away when I put forward to thee the same words which, in such matters, Moses earlier and Jeremiah later put forward to God: the one, I pray, Lord, [0596] saying, I am not sufficient before yesterday nor before the third day, nor since thou didst begin to speak to me: I am weak of voice and slow of tongue; [n: Exod. iv, 10.] the other, O thou who art, Master Lord, saying, I know not how to speak, for I am a youth. [n: Jerem. i, 6.] For in truth I am weak of voice and slow of tongue, and bereft of the intelligence for speaking what is good; having neither a mind that reaches to divine things, nor the word of utterance able to serve the greatness of what was asked. ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ For I am still youthful in the passions, and I have the insurrection of the flesh getting the better of the law of the spirit. ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ And even if we grant, by hypothesis, that I lack nothing of the power for these things, who is so bold and daring as to add ordinances to what has thus been decreed hierarchically by divine decree? ⟦q23.2|19+20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ I beg thee rather to make clear to me in thy precious letters, by a definition, what operation is, and in how many ways operation is said, and what, beside it, belongs to the effect of operation; and what difference we assert of these with respect to the work and to the action, so that I may be able to know the force of what has been written. ⟦q23.2|26⟧ For I have not yet been able to determine exactly which, and how said or understood, one must take the one operation. ⟦q23.2|27⟧ For we do not simply utter meaningless sounds; we signify conceptions by the sounds. ⟦q23.2|28⟧ For which reason I have found the God-speaking Fathers often conceding words, but conceptions never; because the mystery of our salvation lies not in syllables, but in concepts and realities. ⟦q23.2|29⟧ For the one they did, taking thought for peace; the other, establishing souls in the truth. ⟦q23.2|30⟧ So much on these things.

⟦q23.2|31⟧ I beg thy holiness, precious Father, to receive kindly the divine Fathers who bear this modest letter, who have given themselves to this voyage for a need profitable to the common order of monks; and to consent that all be done for them that the exact word of the divine commandment wills for those who aspire to divine things and have the incarnate Word as the one and only pattern of their own life. ⟦q23.2|32⟧ For they are worthy of [0597] your zeal in this, because they draw blamelessly the divine yoke of virtue and of knowledge. ⟦q23.2|33⟧ I fear, writing these things, lest I carry off a reproach of untimely boldness and tastelessness among those who do not know thee, Father, and have had no experience of thy condescension; and lest there be said justly of me too what one of the wise outside our word said, seeing one of the tasteless puffed up over something: A wise man, by smiling, has maddened a fool to insolence. ⟦q23.2|34⟧ In all things, then, holy Father, forgive me thy servant, and by thy all-holy prayers make Christ God propitious to me, whom thou servest continually by purity of mind and exactness of life.

⟦q24.1|1⟧ LETTER 20

⟦q24.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to Marinus the monk.*

⟦q24.1|2⟧ I, knowing the multitude of my own offences, and seeing the condemnation that, as is likely, will receive me for them after the departure from this life; and considering that great and dread tribunal as though already assembled in my soul, before which each of us shall receive, according to desert, the requitals of what we have done, had resolved to keep silence as my housemate, sparing in every way the divine words that are above me, as it seems to me at least; lest I be condemned more, and more justly, as bringing forth words of God put to death by me through my inaction in good works, and therefore unable to furnish to the hearers the life that is in grace, and unable to display the manners of the virtues in the conduct of me who speak; and lest I become liable to the threat to come, as not only having myself, within myself, murdered through my evil manners the life-giving words of God, but also presenting to the hearers the whole evil conduct of my life as a living image, plain to all, of contempt for them. ⟦q24.1|3⟧ I then, as I said, both knowing and fearing these things, shook off my eagerness for speaking and writing the divine words, judging it the greatest injustice, and a ruinous loss, to carry off vain praises from the hearers for things of which I have not the working. ⟦q24.1|4+5⟧ But since the most holy abbot, great in prudence and in all things precious to me, has overcome the verdict of my silence with the many charms of divine thoughts — he whom I could not thrust away, because of the beauty of the virtues that shines in him according to divine intelligence; and he has compelled me to engrave this modest syllable to thy godliness, having related some parts of thy achievements according to God, and having profited me exceedingly — I thought it needful to say in summary those things that are profitable and saving to thee and to me, and to all who wish to hear.

⟦q24.1|4+5⟧ [0600] David, the great prophet and king, showing those who have resolved to love God, and have chosen the divine salvation above all things on earth, the way of the divine virtues that is truly of knowledge, and opening the door of knowledge that lies in practice, says: The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord, and a good intelligence to all who do it. [n: Psal. cx, 10.] If, then, the fear is the beginning of wisdom, the end of wisdom, clearly, is by nature the good intelligence, for those who do it. ⟦q24.1|6+7⟧ And if these things are so, let us be zealous with all our power to acquire the fear of God, and the good intelligence: the fear, that we may flee the defilements of sin, shaking them out of the soul by the expectation of the everlasting punishments; the intelligence, that we may be shown skilled doers of all the divine wills. ⟦q24.1|8⟧ For he who fears the penalties for transgressions of the divine commandments is not caught by the passions that defile his soul; and he who has inwardly the good intelligence, which distinguishes, by the reason that is due, the temporal from the everlasting, becomes luxuriant with all the divine goods, contriving in no way that the knowledge in him be shown idle. ⟦q24.1|9⟧ For as fear, in those who truly fear God, becomes the destroyer of the unclean passions, so also the intelligence of the truly intelligent becomes the craftsman of all the virtues. ⟦q24.1|10⟧ Let us then fear God, and do nothing of what he does not wish; and let us love his intelligence, and neglect to do nothing of what he wishes. ⟦q24.1|10⟧ For he who fears God hates all lawlessness, and he who has made the good intelligence his house-guest loves all righteousness. ⟦q24.1|11⟧ For as hatred of lawlessness signifies the one who fears, so also love of righteousness makes manifest the intelligent man.

⟦q24.1|12⟧ Let us not, then, put on fear in the bearing of dejection alone, nor show our reverence as going no further than the manners that appear; but by a disposition of soul and a humility of heart made fast by frequent groanings in the depth, and struck invisibly in the conscience by the scourge of the memory of former sins, and therefore, by its contraction upon itself, not bearing at all to look at the stumblings of others. ⟦q24.1|13⟧ For he who feigns the fear of God by bearing alone differs not at all from the ape, which imitates the bearing and the postures of men. ⟦q24.1|14⟧ For this animal among the four-footed, standing by nature far from human nature, seems to those who watch it, by its imitation of bearing, to be a man, though it possesses by nature nothing human; just as he too who imitates the bearing of those who truly fear the Lord, to deceive those who see him, but has not the disposition of inclination [0601] imbued, equally with them, by the divine fear, is another Sadducee or Scribe, both being one and being called one: keeping the outward form of reverence, with seemliness from without, but falling altogether short of the reality, because of the unclean friendship for the passions that lies somewhere in the depth of the soul.

⟦q24.1|15⟧ Nor again let us display divine knowledge by the mere utterance of words polished for beauty; nor define the good intelligence as going no further than the bare being able to speak, without doing; but by a disposition of soul imbued through practice with the virtues, and therefore brightening by practice the splendour of knowledge, and by knowledge making radiant the tension of practice; so that fear, coming together through deeds with the longing for God that accords with the good intelligence, may work the divine wisdom perfect in us: beginning in the abstention from evils that accords with the divine fear, and brought to perfection in the practice of the commandments according to the good intelligence. ⟦q24.1|16+17+18⟧ For he who defines intelligence as lying in the mere bare utterance of the divine words is like a parrot mimicking human words. ⟦q24.1|16+17+18⟧ For this animal, alone among the birds, being taught, feigns human words, though it has by nature nothing at all human; just as he too who, going no further than bare utterance, mimics the words of the truly intelligent to astonish the hearers, but has not the habit of knowledge imbued with the practice of the virtues, is truly another Pharisee, or a blameworthy priest, both being one and being called one; feigning intelligence by the mere utterance of words, but falling altogether short of the wisdom itself that truly has its being in deeds; and therefore greatly puffed up, and, by the boastfulness of the conceit that nourishes the pride of his mind, standing against the word of the truth; not knowing, it seems, that knowledge without practice, in those who are greatly puffed up over it for love of glory, is manifestly a theology of demons, having Jesus expressly rebuking it [n: Luc. iv, 33.] — the one of whom it would speak as God; even if such a man, held fast by the passion, does not perceive it.

⟦q24.1|16+17+18⟧ Therefore let us fear God with fear, and free ourselves from every defilement of flesh and spirit, not allowing the law of the flesh, through the praised mortification of the members, to rise up against the law of the spirit; nor let us circumscribe the magnificence of the divine fear by the manners that appear in bearing alone, but let us make the composure of our bearing a herald of the good disposition within, in the soul, that we may flee the coming threat of the fire; and let us adorn the good intelligence with the practice of the commandments, that we may not, like the vainglorious, carry about dead words, bereft of the life that lies in the practice of the commandments; but let us [0604] make the divine words effective in practice, pursuing peace with all, and love; caring for kindness and meekness, and long-suffering, and forbearance in all things toward all; bearing what befalls with patience, and with thanksgiving; and receiving afflictions unshaken as divine gifts; in nothing resisting the principles of the providence that, through these things, conducts our life profitably; so that we may attain everlasting life with all the saints, delighting in the goods to come, in the abiding of the blessedness of everlasting things, hearing God say to us: Come, you blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world; [n: Matth. xxv, 34.] and may we rejoice, enjoying those ineffable goods which eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor have they entered into the heart of man, which God has prepared for those who love him. [n: I Cor. ii, 9.]

⟦q24.1|16+17+18⟧ LETTER 21

⟦q24.1|16+17+18⟧ *Of the same, to the most holy bishop of Cydonia.*

⟦q24.2|1+2⟧ Having come to know, through pious inquiry into the mystery of his economy toward us, that God himself is equal to himself in height and in depth, my God-guarded master, having marvelled how the limit and the limitlessness — contraries and unmixed, joined together in him — had their manifestation through one another, the infinity being ineffably limited together with the limit, and the limit being extended, beyond nature, together with the infinity; while he himself, about whom these things are, is in both the same with himself, having in no way at all lowered himself as regards the infinite, as being by nature unlimited, and by economy both being and being known as incomprehensible; having the height of the divine glory beyond thought, and the depth of the condescension of the economy uncontainable — wisely submitted himself, pliantly, like wax, to God as to a seal; and receiving him impressed wholly into his depth, he made himself a conspicuous imitation of the divine blessedness. ⟦q24.2|3⟧ For, wisely exchanging himself into the divine modes, with the lowliness of nature he blended, through virtue, the eminence of dignity; and with the height of dignity, conversely, he raised up together, through knowledge, the humility of nature; and he has made each to be contemplated, paradoxically, in the other. ⟦q24.2|4+5⟧ For in both he has kept himself the same with himself, having lowered nothing of his solemnity in either; showing very clearly, through the things by which he acquired the unadulterated imitation of God, that God put forward the priesthood on earth, ordaining it in his own stead, so that, even while it is seen bodily, it may not fail to make his mysteries apparent to those able to see.

⟦q24.2|4+5⟧ [0605] For this reason my master, bearing himself in a manner like God's, by word together with manners, has wrought much salvation in me. ⟦q24.2|6+7⟧ For as the Lord besought men to be reconciled to God and Father as though they had been wronged rather than had done wrong — as the divine Paul testifies, saying, We are ambassadors for Christ, as though God were beseeching through us: we pray you on behalf of Christ, be reconciled to God [n: II Cor. v, 20.] — so that he might shame them, by the greatness of the benefit, into coming to an awareness of their own hardening; so also my master, through a voluntary lowering, skilfully overpowering me, by seeming to wish to learn from me through letters the things he has been appointed to teach because of his dignity, has made me, though late, aware of my own ignorance. ⟦q24.2|8⟧ I confess, then, my thanks to my master; and I glory in the manner by which he gently thrust away the cloud of my conceit; and I ask him not to cease setting his servant in order by such words and manners.

⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ LETTER 22

⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ *Of the same, to Auxentius.*

⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ If, trusting in the Spirit — inasmuch as you have love clasped about you indissolubly, needing nothing for renewal — you have decided to neglect letters to your friends, I both praise the rule and approve of you, as entrusting the treasure of love to him from whom and into whom it is wont both to begin and to come to rest. ⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ But if, making light of the fitting manner of friendship, you excuse yourselves from writing out of sloth, I shall not cease to do the contrary, not knowing how to stand in awe of friends because of the greatness of their standing in the world. ⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ And if neither of the two is the cause of not writing, receive from the absent, and give readily to the absent, as nature has taught, converse through letters — or rather, as God, making nature wise, has consoled it. ⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ For speech shaped in letters goads the memory when it is at rest, and rouses it when it nods, to see and to take the impress of the faces of those who are loved; through which longing, like a fire that has been quenched, is fanned up again from a small spark.

⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ LETTER 23

⟦q24.3|1+2+3+4+5⟧ *Of the same, to Stephen the presbyter.*

⟦q24.4|1+2⟧ It is proper to spiritual love not only to do good to those in need when present, but also to console them when absent; and not to allow souls to be cut apart along with bodies; nor indeed the power of reason, through which the soul bears the image of the Creator, to be [0608] circumscribed by place; but either, when present, to converse with the beloved by voice, face to face, on fitting things, or, when absent, to speak with them through letters. ⟦q24.4|3⟧ For nature, by the grace of God, wisely devised this manner, for an unseparated union of those who stand far apart from one another bodily by local distance. ⟦q24.4|4⟧ For so speech, sounding in us the more frequently by voice and by letters, sets in the soul a memory hard to wash out, so as to see continually those who are ever present in the spirit by love, and to embrace them, and to be called back from all grievous things. ⟦q24.4|5⟧ Do not, then, forget me, your child and disciple, precious Fathers, but, as being both disciples and teachers of love, feed my starving soul with the word of the virtues, and enlighten my mind, pressed down by the gloom of ignorance, looking to the reward laid up for you in heaven for this; even if what is done should perhaps draw you away for a little from a better and more precious leisure. ⟦q24.4|6⟧ For I know that it is not equal in good repute to converse with God without intermediary and to provide with sympathy for the lesser. ⟦q24.4|7⟧ Yet neither is this alien to the things held lawful with God.

⟦q25.1|1⟧ LETTER 24

⟦q25.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to Constantine the Sacellarius.*

⟦q25.1|2⟧ The honoured letter of my God-guarded master came, with peace, bearing himself whole, appearing in his manners. ⟦q25.1|3⟧ For with the dignity of the words the disposition of the soul too is somehow wont to be reflected, together with the gentleness of the manners. ⟦q25.1|4⟧ And I rejoiced as though I had received my master himself present, and I embraced him in the spirit, and, enfolding him whole in the room of my heart, I laid him up there; and I glorified Christ our God, who wisely implanted in men the law of love, according to which those who know how duly to tend the seeds of love can never be absent from one another, even though they stand far apart from one another in body by the distance of place. ⟦q25.1|5⟧ But since the letter, bringing good tidings of worldly peace, changed the soul's grief into joy, it is fitting that we, for this peace, should with good reason become in every way grateful to God who granted it, lest we appear ungrateful for the kindness, repaying the benefactor ill. ⟦q25.1|6⟧ And we shall become in every way grateful by using the peace as is fitting, [0609] and by setting aside the friendship with the world and the world-ruler which has ill come to be in us; and let us dissolve, even if late, the war set up against God through the passions, and, having made indissoluble treaties of peace with him in the abolishing of the body, [n: Rom. vi, 6.] cease from our enmity toward him. ⟦q25.1|7⟧ For it is impossible for us to be made friends with God while we rebel against him through the passions, and consent through vice to pay tribute to the wicked tyrant and murderer of souls, the devil, unless we have first become wholly at war with the wicked one. ⟦q25.1|8⟧ For so long do we stand as enemies and foes of God — even though we mould about ourselves the name of believers — as we will to be slaves to the passions of dishonour; [n: Rom. i, 26.] and no profit will any longer accrue to us from the peace of the world, while the soul is ill disposed, and rebels against its own Maker, and does not consent to come under his kingship, but is still sold to ten thousand cruel masters, who press it on into vice and contrive that it deceitfully choose the road that leads to destruction rather than the one that saves. ⟦q25.1|9⟧ But when we sober up from the drunkenness of the passions, and rise up against the wicked and tyrannical kingship of the devil, and shake off from ourselves his most bitter yoke, and see how greatly the friendship with him has corrupted us — then we shall receive the peaceful coming of Christ, the peaceful and meek King, taking place invisibly upon us; and we shall consent to come under his yoke, as he orders and composes us by laws of truth; and we shall accept willingly to contract friendship with him, bringing him willingly tributes and taxes: the fruits of righteousness brought to perfection in us through the virtues according to the commandment. ⟦q25.1|10⟧ Delighting in these for the sake of our salvation, he who alone is without need will, as one who does not lie, grant us all that he has promised us. ⟦q25.1|11+12⟧ And these are: What eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it gone up into the heart of man, what God has prepared for those who love him. [n: I Cor. ii, 9.] For to this end he also made us: that we might become partakers of the divine nature [n: II Petr. i, 4.] and sharers in his eternity, and might appear like him according to the deification that is from grace — for whose sake is the whole constitution and continuance of the things that are, and the bringing forth and coming-to-be of the things that are not.

⟦q25.1|13⟧ Let us become worthy, then, of the peace, my blessed master, manfully stripping from ourselves the passions within us that rebel against God. ⟦q25.1|14⟧ Let us waste the body as far as we have power, through which, when it swells with vigour, the devil's entry into the soul naturally comes about. ⟦q25.1|15⟧ With [0612] divine readings let us halt the wandering mind, and lead it back again to God. ⟦q25.1|16⟧ With the tensions of self-control and vigil let us temperately restrain desire, carried in disorder by appetite toward many things. ⟦q25.1|17⟧ With prayers and supplications and almsgiving let us wisely lull anger, boiling and troubled. ⟦q25.1|18⟧ With the fear of God and the threat of what is to come let us stop the sense-organs, moved and operating licentiously. ⟦q25.1|19⟧ Upon all things, and before all, let us remember death, and the fearful going-out of the soul from the body; and how the principalities and authorities and powers of darkness meet it in this air, each dragging it apart toward itself and cutting it up, in proportion to the evil kinship that came to be, relationally, toward it through the passion in between. ⟦q25.1|20+21+22⟧ Let us remember the bitter pain that comes upon the soul in Hades according to conscience, at the memory of the things it did ill through the body. ⟦q25.1|20+21+22⟧ Let us remember the last consummation of the whole world, in which all this is burned up with much and immense fire: the elements fearfully shaken with the melting of the burning; heaven, in its rolling up [and] in fear, fleeing in haste from the face of the fire that cleanses creation beforehand because of the coming of the Pure One; the sea vanishing; the earth seething, and from its foundation [?], and yielding up undiminished the infinite myriads of human bodies. ⟦q25.1|20+21+22⟧ Let us remember the awful hour of the defence that will take place before the fearful and awful judgement-seat of Christ, when all the power of the heavens and all the creation of men from the foundation of the world looks upon what belongs to each, down to a bare thought; when the unutterable light will receive some, because of the brightness of their works, and the illumination of the holy and blessed Trinity, shining forth more clearly, for those able through purity of soul to see and receive it; and the outer darkness will receive others, because of the outrageousness of their works, and the sleepless worm, and the unquenchable fire of Gehenna, and — heaviest of all — the shame in the conscience that has no end. ⟦q25.1|20+21+22⟧ Let us remember all these things, that we may become worthy of the one, and may not be condemned to make trial of the other; and let us become our own and God's — or rather, of God alone and whole, and ourselves whole — bearing nothing earthly in ourselves, that we may draw near to God and become gods, receiving from God our being gods. ⟦q25.1|23⟧ For thus are the divine gifts honoured, and the presence of the divine peace welcomed with friendliness. ⟦q25.1|24⟧ And so much on these matters. ⟦q25.1|25⟧ And may the God of peace, who reconciled us to himself through the cross, and ransomed us with his own blood from the authority of darkness, himself grant his peace that surpasses every [0613] mind to my blessed master, and count him worthy of his heavenly kingdom, where is the dwelling of all who rejoice, and the choir of those who keep festival. [n: Psal. xli, 5.]

⟦q25.1|25⟧ LETTER 25

⟦q25.2|1+2+3⟧ *Of the same, to Conon, presbyter and abbot.*

⟦q25.2|1+2+3⟧ I believe, as I received and was taught, that God is love; and that, as he himself is one, never ceasing from being one, so he makes one those who live according to his love, and grants them one heart and soul, even though they happen to be many; [n: I Joan. iv, 16.] so that, as having one soul, they may know one another's hearts, and may not toil in ignorance searching about things unseen, each conjecturing his neighbour's disposition toward himself. ⟦q25.2|1+2+3⟧ Therefore, trusting in the grace of Christ that moves in thee, holy Father, that thou art ignorant of nothing of what is in my heart — as perhaps, though it is bold to say, neither am I of what is in thee — I take courage to excuse myself for the time being, at present, from my coming to you, knowing that you believe the excuse to be not of disobedience but of a passion of the body, forcibly hindering me from the road and not allowing the soul's eagerness to come forward into deed. ⟦q25.2|4⟧ But, absent in body through the present letter, I who am ever present in the spirit, and never separated from you in soul, embrace thee, my father according to God and protector, and the guardian of my salvation, and thy holy flock, worthy of the kingdom of Christ; and I beg that supplications be made for me to the Lord, that I may carry away my faith in him living and effective in deed, not deadened by the passions, but having the power of his cross and death, and of his burial and resurrection: of the cross, by the inaction of sin; of death, by the fullest casting off of vice; of burial, by the putting away of the soul's imagination concerning sensible things; of resurrection, by the wealth of the virtues, and the abundance of the true full knowledge of him, and the raising of the understanding on high from the things that perish — that I may become of one body with him and of one soul. ⟦q25.2|5⟧ And, to speak simply, according to his promise, like him in all things, apart from identity with him according to nature; lest, having deadened by the works of dishonour the life given me by him through the things said, I justly incur the sentence of those who killed their own life, our Lord Jesus Christ. ⟦q25.2|6⟧ For truly fearful, and beyond all condemnation, is it willingly to deaden the life given us by God through the gift of the Holy Spirit, through love for the things that perish. ⟦q25.2|7⟧ And they know this fear well who have practised preferring the truth to self-love.

⟦q25.2|7⟧ [0616] LETTER 26

⟦q25.3|1⟧ *Of the same, to Thalassius the presbyter, who asked how some of the kings among the nations, on account of a God-sent wrath lying upon the subject multitude, sacrificed children and kinsmen, and the wrath ceased, as is recorded by many of the ancients.*

⟦q25.3|2+3+4⟧ To the Greeks too God permitted the receiving of oracles, through which they devised for themselves modes of foreknowledge of things to come and of deliverance from what grieved them; and he often granted things to turn out for them according to their hopes, preparing the way for all by providence — as each was able to receive it — for the acceptance of the great mystery to come. ⟦q25.3|2+3+4⟧ For if the affection of rulers for those they rule, advancing into deed, snatched the whole subject people out of the necessity lying upon it, it is nothing strange or incredible if he who is by nature the lover of man, and alone Creator and King of men, offered his own Son unto death for all men, by way of economy, through flesh, that he might ransom them from the evils that held them. ⟦q25.3|2+3+4⟧ Of course: when the kings of Israel and Judah had marched against the Moabites in the time of the holy prophet Elisha, and after a great victory were about to sack the very royal city itself, the whole of it, the king of the country, taking his son — according to what was customary, as it seems, among the nations — sacrificed him on the wall before the eyes of the kings; and in this manner he appeased the kings and persuaded them to turn back from the [0617] wrath prepared against him, and saved his subjects.

⟦q25.3|5⟧ To the Greeks, then, God, by permitting them to perform what came of their own law, wisely made them his own for the acceptance of the great mystery; to the Israelite people, by giving a law, he prefigured throughout the mystery to come. ⟦q25.3|6⟧ Hence, after the mystery of our salvation had appeared and come to be in operation, he abolished both these things and those, from Gentiles and from Jews, whatever he had given before, or had permitted to come about, for the prefiguring of what was to come. ⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ For from him, and through him, and to him are all things, [n: Rom. XI, 36.] whatever has come to be or will come to be, at every time or in every mode: some by good pleasure, some by economy, some by permission. ⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ What the ancients record, then, is not a myth: it truly happened, and the remedy for which it was done followed upon it. ⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ The detailed account of what has been written let us exercise together, with God, when I am with you. ⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ Only, he who has known how even the flesh of a serpent can by art heal the sick will accept with faith the method of divine providence.

⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ LETTER 27

⟦q25.3|7+8+9+10⟧ *Of the same, to John the Cubicularius.*

⟦q25.4|1+2⟧ Those who, according to the world, have established love toward one another need one another's bodily presence for its preservation, because of forgetfulness, which naturally withers every longing that is set up about bodies alone; and with it the memory of those so disposed toward one another is wont to be quenched as well. ⟦q25.4|3⟧ And sometimes satiety, supervening, sets apart the very thing that seems able to preserve the worldly relation toward one another, and wipes out the whole disposition that went before. ⟦q25.4|4⟧ And often the failing of some one of the causes that supply it, or even some small pretext falling in by the way, makes swift the turning into hatred of a love so constituted. ⟦q25.4|5⟧ But those who according to God have been bound to one another indissolubly by the bond of love, whether they are with one another or absent from one another, tighten its bond the more, having it in themselves because of God, who is love, and who provides to the worthy the power of being able to love — and very justly, as I think. ⟦q25.4|6⟧ For with things by nature fluid, the disposition of those disposed toward one another by them is wont to flow away as well; but with things stable and holding ever the same, the affection of those disposed toward one another by them is wont to abide with them, altogether unshaken and unbudging, stretching their longing for one another toward the boundless as far as God, the cause of this relation, is by nature able to draw to himself those who hang upon one another in the spirit.

⟦q25.4|6⟧ [0620] Loving thee, then, in this manner, most beloved, I have thee always inseparable, present with me in soul, stamped deep in the spirit — though the length of time is long, and the distance of the places between is great — never ceasing to see thee intelligibly and to embrace thee. ⟦q25.4|7⟧ And believing myself loved by thee equally, if not even more, I do not shrink from writing without hesitation about what is needful, knowing that one soul has come to be of us both through the Spirit. ⟦q25.4|8⟧ And I know that thou dost not think thyself insulted in hearing this, reckoning the condescension of God toward men, fearful and above mind and reason. ⟦q25.4|9⟧ Receive kindly, then, him who brings this present modest syllable, and become to him all that he enjoined who made it law for men to share out among themselves one another's misfortunes, and to make the neighbour so much one's own that he is not thought another, according to the disposition exchanged between those who have been changed into one another. ⟦q25.4|10⟧ For not only to rejoice with those who rejoice, but also to weep with those who weep; and not simply to love, but to love the neighbour as oneself, the divine word enjoins [n: Rom. XII, 15.] — not to mention the new commandment, which enjoins laying down even our very lives for one another for love's sake, when it is the time to contend; [n: I Joan. III, 16.] which the Lord who gave it fulfilled in deed, not refusing to lay down his own life for us: for us a pattern of perfect well-pleasing, by which the law of self-love is quietly quenched — the law which at the beginning, through pleasure, deceitfully led us away from God and from one another; and, having taught that instead of one God many are to be thought to exist, or none at all, utterly dashed down the power of the reason in us to the flesh, and, cutting the one nature into many portions, made bestial against one another, for pleasure's sake, the power of the anger in us. ⟦q25.4|11⟧ Become, then, as I said, all things to him who needs thy help, that thou mayest find God becoming such to thee — he who for love for man consents to become all things to all.

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ LETTER 28

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ *Of the same, to Cyrisicius the bishop.*

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ Having been allotted, honoured Father, according to the grace of the [0621] high-priesthood given thee, to be on earth an imitator of the divine goodness, thou hast left none of the manners that characterize it unfulfilled; but, having them all following one upon another in order, thou hast been zealous to adorn the mystery of the high-priesthood with the tunic of the virtues, woven from above through the Spirit [n: Joan. XIX, 23.] — which tunic the demons can never rend, not even by a multitude of temptations, though they crucify you. Be zealous, then, to gather into one the scattered children of God. [n: Joan. XI, 52.] For this too is a characteristic mark of the divine goodness; and, having become as head of the honoured body of the holy Church of God, fit the members together with one another through the architecture of the Spirit; and, appointed herald of the divine teachings, call with a loud voice both those far off and those near; and bind them to thyself with the indissoluble bond of the love of the Spirit — if, to be sure, the expectation of the enemies has fled and has utterly vanished, on account of which they have been estranged so long from the land that bore them — so that thou too mayest be able to say, as befits Christ: Behold I, and the children whom God has given me. But count thy lowly and unworthy servant and suppliant worthy of thy honoured syllables on this matter, holy Father; and do not disdain, offering a sacrifice of praise, to commend him to God through prayers.

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ LETTER 29

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ God, seeking, came down from heaven, and, longing for the sheep, put it on; and so, having saved the wandering one, placing it upon his shoulders, he brought it back with compassion to its fellows of the pasture. [n: Matth. XVIII, 12; Luc. XV, 4.] But thou, all-holy Father and shepherd, only filling with the Holy Spirit the reed-pipe of thy teaching, hast brought back to thyself complete the herd of rational sheep, which unwillingly departed from its homeland, but willingly runs to thy voice, the voice of the good and skilled shepherd, as having perfect discernment of what is profitable. For it did not cross the long seas wandering, having disdained the divine pasture, but for the sake of a safety that preserves the divine offerings of the soul. But receive a flock that has not only remained unapproachable to wolves but has even become a destroyer of wolves, and is rich in many goods of the soul. And stretch forth, and prosper, and reign, because of truth and meekness and righteousness, [n: Psal. XLIV, 5.] as teacher of the divine doctrines of piety, and guide of a pure life and a dignified [0624] conduct, and lawgiver of right discernment in all things; receiving especially, together with the said holy flock, also the sanctified lord abba George the presbyter, a holy man, possessing a life equal to his word, and through both a destroyer of the wicked power. But since I have been swallowed up by great grief, not bearing the separation from these, either receive me too and give me rest under your wings, or strengthen me through prayers to bear nobly the pain at the separation from those dearest.

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ LETTER 30

⟦q26.1|1+2+3+4⟧ *Of the same, to John the bishop.* ⟦q26.2|1+2+3+4+5⟧

⟦q26.3|1+2⟧ Those who have examined by reason the nature of the things that are say that the power of fire draws to itself every subject; and the interpreters of the divine mysteries, comparing God symbolically to it, say that he too draws all who wish to obey his laws and who consent to embrace living holily; and, saying that the priesthood is, as in an image, a painting that aptly has the imitation of him, they declare that it too, by the equal law of sympathy according to grace, draws all who are under the same nature. ⟦q26.3|3⟧ Thou, then, holy Father, having been allotted to have on earth this image of God, call to thyself by manners and words of sympathy those who long to return to thee and desire, as in their prayers, to be shepherded by thee; and confirm with true promises, through thy honoured syllables, thy providence toward them in all things, with God — if the foul expectation of the enemies has utterly ceased, on account of which those endured so great a flight who held it safe to follow the law of God, which cries expressly: If they persecute you out of this city, flee into the other; [n: Matth. X, 23.] and who preferred a praiseworthy cowardice to rashness — so that, trusting, after God, in thy words, they may make their return without fear, and may find, as rest from the toils by which, for virtue's sake and for obeying God who bids them flee their persecutors, they were bruised in this fearful sojourn abroad, thy merciful support of them in all things. ⟦q26.3|4⟧ Do not then disdain, holy Father, to receive thy suppliant and servant who beseeches thee; but honour with thine own syllables, as one honourable, him who is worthy of no honour; and through prayers commend him to Christ, who alone is honourable, and who through himself honoured most honourably man, who was without honour; imitating whom in all things as Master and Lord, thou hast made thyself, by the grace of the Spirit, another such as he to those who see thee.

⟦q26.4|1⟧ LETTER 31

⟦q26.4|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.4|2⟧ As a ray gently draws the healthy [0625] sight, which by nature delights in light, and gives it a share of its own brightness, so the true priesthood too, being through all things a characteristic mark of the blessed Godhead for those on earth, draws to itself every soul that is God-loving and divine in its habit, and gives it a share of its own knowledge, peace and love; that, having brought each power of the soul to the end of its own operation, it may present to God, deified through all things, those whom it initiates into the mysteries. ⟦q26.4|3⟧ For the end of the soul's rational operation is true knowledge; of the desiring, love; of the incensive, peace; as also the end of the true priesthood is, through these, to be made god and to make god. ⟦q26.4|4⟧ For it is to this end that we have by nature reasoning, to seek God; and we received desire, to long for him alone; and we were given anger, to contend for him alone. ⟦q26.4|5+6⟧ And the end of the seeking is true knowledge; of the longing, unbroken and fiery love; of the divine contests, the peace that surpasses every mind. [n: Philipp. IV, 7.] Knowing you, all-holy and all-blessed, to be doers and teachers of these highest goods, she who is truly the servant of God, and daughter and mother, the lady Eudocia the recluse, has returned to you with her holy flock, showing you, honoured by God, victors over all who were zealous to have her with themselves. ⟦q26.4|7⟧ For she is truly a servant, as one who fulfils the divine commandments; a daughter, as having through all things in her habit an invariant likeness to God; a mother, as one who also begets in others the habit of virtue. ⟦q26.4|8⟧ Rejoice, then, henceforth and exult, having taken back the more honourable part of your holy flock; and especially having received too my sanctified lord George the presbyter, truly God's tilled field, and the most approved husbandman of the divine and great George, competent in deed and word before God and all the people in the science of the husbandry of souls, and for this reason lovely to all who love the Lord. ⟦q26.4|9⟧ But since meanwhile I have become half-dead and cut in half, torn off from their profitable companionship, either receive me too as a sickened member and heal me, or console me through prayers and persuade me to bear the separation dispassionately. ⟦q26.4|10⟧ For this too is proper to the divine and true priesthood.

⟦q26.5|1⟧ LETTER 32

⟦q26.5|1⟧ *Of the same, to abba Polychronius.*

⟦q26.5|2+3+4+5⟧ Since we are about to keep the feast of the burial and resurrection of Christ, who was put to death and buried for us according to the flesh, let us examine whether we too have been put to death and buried according to the old man, who is corrupted according to the desires of deceit, and have risen according to the new man, who is [0628] renewed according to the image of him who created him. [n: Ephes. IV, 22-24.] The sign of the putting to death of the first is complete freedom from the passions; of the resurrection of the second, the spotless and true boldness of conscience, and the illumination of knowledge in the mind. ⟦q26.5|2+3+4+5⟧ And if these things have come to be in us, let us keep the feast with Christ God; but if not, let us strive at least not to betray the Word to the passions like Judas, or not to deny him like Peter; lest perhaps we be unable to repent, and be led away regretting without profit, or to weep bitterly and wash off the brands of the denial. ⟦q26.5|2+3+4+5⟧ For denial of the Word is the refusal, through fear, to do the good; and betrayal is the purposed impulse toward sin in operation: of which may it not befall us to have experience.

⟦q26.5|2+3+4+5⟧ LETTER 33

⟦q26.5|2+3+4+5⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.6|1+2+3+4⟧ If, according to Job, the great victor over temptations, The life of men is a trial; [n: Job VII, 1.] and the world, as the Lord said, lies in the evil one; [n: I Joan. V, 19.] and, Many are the afflictions of the righteous, as the great David declares; [n: Psal. XXXIII, 20.] and we must by all means have the temptations and the wrestling, whether against men or against demons, according to the just judgement of God, as the holy Apostle says [n: Ephes. VI, 12.] — let us beseech God to give patience, that we may be able to be drawn up out of the mire of the passions and to stand upon the unshaken rock — I mean the dispassion in Christ through grace — there having been put to death in us the life that tempts, and the world that does evil, and men, and demons: that is, the passionate life, and the pleasurable deceit of sense, and the fleshly sufferings, and the passionate movements of the soul, and the involuntary inflictions that come upon these, which have been called afflictions. ⟦q26.6|1+2+3+4⟧ Let us endure, then, that we may be saved; for he who endures to the end shall be saved. ⟦q26.6|1+2+3+4⟧ The peace of Christ be with us. ⟦q26.6|1+2+3+4⟧ Amen.

⟦q26.7|1⟧ LETTER 34

⟦q26.7|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.7|2+3⟧ Going away to his passion, the Lord left us peace; and again, having risen from the dead, he gave us peace. [n: Joan. XIV, 27; Joan. 20, 19.] Hinting at what, through these? ⟦q26.7|4⟧ That we must carry ourselves through contrary seasons dispassionately: neither, falling under grievous circumstances, to be without the better hope, nor, carried round with bright occasions, to thrust away the thought that tutors us; but to be the same, both suffering and faring well. ⟦q26.7|5⟧ And this does not come about unless we first tread upon the asp and the basilisk — that is, rise above disobedience to the divine words and the acceptance of wicked thoughts — and trample the lion and the dragon, [n: Psal. XC, 13.] that is, [0629] anger and desire; which being done away, all peace will be ours, nothing being able to move us toward any of the things that abide here. ⟦q26.7|6⟧ Let us master anger and desire, and we have cast down the whole house of the foreigners at once, together with those in it, killing together with the passions the passionate life and way of living.

⟦q26.8|1⟧ LETTER 35

⟦q26.8|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.8|2⟧ If, after that great and fearful desert, the Israelite people receives the land of rest; and — the most astounding report of all — God, who became man for our sake, being also Lord of all, enters into his glory through sufferings and cross and death, there being no other mode appointed for him, as it is written: Ought not Christ to have suffered these things, and to enter into his glory? [n: Luc. XXIV, 26.] — let us not lose heart, growing faint in afflictions; but let us look to the author of our salvation and perfecter, Jesus, [n: Hebr. XII, 2.] and let us bear nobly what befalls us. ⟦q26.8|3+4+5⟧ For the end of every affliction is joy; and of every toil, rest; and of every dishonour, glory; and, simply, the end of all the pains borne for virtue is to be with God always, and to enjoy the everlasting relief that has no end. ⟦q26.8|3+4+5⟧ But pray, Father, that we may attain it, having crossed the sea of life well, and having kept the intelligible footsteps of the soul unwetted by its brine.

⟦q26.8|3+4+5⟧ LETTER 36

⟦q26.8|3+4+5⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q26.9|1+2⟧ Blessed be our master to the Lord, and blessed the garden of his heart, which Jesus, the true husbandman, planted and has filled with immortal plants of righteousness; of whose fruits we too have partaken, filled with his spiritual vegetables — with which the great Apostle too knows how to nourish those too weak for the unmediated beholding of divine things, [n: Rom. XIV, 2.] feeding them, as with vegetables, with the natural contemplation of visible things, and through the magnificence of the things that appear leading them by the hand to its Creator. ⟦q26.9|1+2⟧ Imitating him, our blessed Father and teacher too has affectionately entertained us, setting before us symbolically, through the vegetables, the understanding of this creation. ⟦q26.9|3⟧ But give a share of solid food too, and stir our soul, slackened by pleasures and without appetite for divine things, astringing its aspiration with the subtler principle of providence and judgement; that thou mayest not nourish thy disciples with vegetables only, teaching only the principles of the things that come to be and pass away; but with wheat and wine and oil too — the principles of the things eternal — strengthen them: giving the principle that holds the virtues together as firm bread, and with the principle of the unforgetting [0632] knowledge, as with wine, warming the heart of thy followers to a god-making ecstasy; and, as oil, implanting in thy servants the peaceful and untroubled habit of meekness, which nourishes, and keeps unquenched, the flame in them.

⟦q27.1|1⟧ LETTER 37

⟦q27.1|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q27.1|2⟧ Again we have received thy honoured letter, holy Father, with gifts; and again we have enjoyed goods equal to the former. ⟦q27.1|2⟧ By the gifts we quenched the burning of the passions that blackens our soul — for before them we had eaten the intelligible principles, not believing that these had been sent by thee at random and in vain and as it chanced, since thou movest in all things with reason and spirit, and makest even the food of the body a lesson of the soul for us; and by the letter, having found humility, the mother of all the virtues, through which both the coming-to-be of good things and their guarding are wrought for those who have it, we wept abundantly, perceiving how far we have been removed from this road. ⟦q27.1|3+4+5⟧ For thy mourning has become a reproof of the evils in which we are held; and it awoke our understanding, which was deeply asleep, and stripped bare our memory, covered with the veil of puffing-up conceit; and it has made us knowers of ourselves, perceiving in due measure the vexation of our own sins. ⟦q27.1|3+4+5⟧ And in some measure it has made us sharers of wisdom, having received discernment of our own offences — if he is true who says: Those who are knowers of themselves are wise. [n: Prov. XIII, 10.] Do not cease, then, honoured Father, renewing our understanding by word, and asking by prayer deliverance from evils for us; that thou mayest receive as the reward for these, from God who gives it, the perfect cleansing of the soul in conscience.

⟦q27.1|3+4+5⟧ LETTER 38

⟦q27.1|3+4+5⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q27.2|1+2⟧ We thank thee, honoured Father, that thou hast given thyself to us as a pattern of excellent repentance, persuading us through confession to lay bare the dark treasures of the passions within ourselves; through which the hatred of sin is wont to be confirmed. ⟦q27.2|3⟧ For we in truth are they who cherish the passions, whose memory after the deed brings disgust: loving, as onions, the assent to wicked thoughts, by which the soul's power of sight grows duller toward virtue and knowledge, taking on, as it were, scales — the passionate imaginations of visible things. ⟦q27.2|4⟧ For the onion, according to those who give etymologies, is a closing of the pupil. ⟦q27.2|5⟧ And, as garlic, chewing the fulfilment of the passions through operation, by which the mind, being filled with uncleanness, breathes upon those outside, through shameful words and manners, the stench of the evils it has eaten through practice. ⟦q27.2|6⟧ But teach [0633] us, holy Father, how, after casting these off, we shall love the manna — I mean the rational bread of divine knowledge, rained on us from heaven — and keep it incorrupt: neither treasuring it up for the morrow by delay, lest it boil up worms of fleshly passions, rotting through idleness; nor parading it in display, lest it vanish, melting away with vainglory. ⟦q27.2|7⟧ With such words nourish us again, honoured Father, thy disciples, and commend us through prayer to God, us who without thy mediation are unable to worship.

⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ LETTER 39

⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ Ungrudgingly thou nourishest, sanctified Father, both our visible and our intelligible man: supplying the body with what makes for its sustenance, and giving the soul what makes for growth in the spirit. ⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ For God has made thee rich in both, blessing thy coming in [?]. ⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ By coming in I mean the possession of the gifts of grace supplied to thee by God, multiplied through practice; and by going out, the distribution of the word that comes about from thee to others, bearing in those who receive it, as fruit, the operation of what is fitting.

⟦q27.3|1+2+3⟧ LETTER 40

⟦q27.4|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same Thalassius, presbyter and abbot.*

⟦q27.4|2⟧ Having read carefully the wise and honoured letter of your paternal holiness, master, and having examined everything, and read through the whole book of my conscience, I found none of the things it contains in me …; at once I contracted the tension of my soul, binding tight its slackness, judging it vain to be added to the praises of others' goods, and judging it proper only to a soul that has its propensity toward every alluring deception. ⟦q27.4|3⟧ For truly it is vain for one who does not have, in reality, the glory of the things said, to try rashly to appropriate praises as though full of virtue. ⟦q27.4|4+5+6+7⟧ But my master, judging the things of others from his own advantages of virtue, gives his vote — through an abundance of godlike perfection covering the shame — for one wounded by many … of passions, as is the custom of those who love the Lord. ⟦q27.4|4+5+6+7⟧ But I, my [0636] blessed master, … of all-shining perfection in virtue, the command … most ready, being about perhaps to fall into every reproach and blame, as for my rashness in this, from the …, setting your love higher … to the lord abba Thomas; readily serving … this matter: holding out, that is, the material — sufficient, as I think, through the winter — so as to be able, with the Lord working with us, to fulfil this command of yours. ⟦q27.4|4+5+6+7⟧ Fare thou very well for me, beloved and honoured Father, and keep safe by thy prayers to God … thy household servant and slave — and I will add, lover — delivering him by thine own mediation from every diabolical device.

⟦q27.5|1⟧ LETTER 41

⟦q27.5|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q27.5|2+3+4⟧ One who has been taught by the Spirit to take everything captive to Christ knows how to reap benefit from all things; as you too, then, do from me, who am utterly estranged from divine grace and stoutly bound by the laws of the flesh. ⟦q27.5|2+3+4⟧ And having nothing [?] and earnestly, it were needful for me to decline altogether going anywhere at all, lest I do more harm in doing your bidding. ⟦q27.5|2+3+4⟧ And I come when the Lord wills, knowing that you have remained undefiled and unpolluted even while keeping company with me, the profane. ⟦q27.5|5⟧ For what is holy by habit, as by nature, will be stained by nothing unclean. ⟦q27.5|6⟧ As for the vestitor's business, you do well to sympathize, if the matter carries no harm; and especially if you know that he can bear with it.

⟦q27.5|6⟧ LETTER 42

⟦q27.6|1⟧ *Of the same, to the same.*

⟦q27.6|2+3+4⟧ The blessed David, that great king of the Lord, after the fearful wars of the foreigners, after the great temptations from those of his own race, after the toilsome flights and the frequent plots in return for his many benefactions, after the uprisings of his kinsmen, after the … and the kingship, when he perceived God … him on every side — then he purposed to build a house to the Lord God …. ⟦q27.6|2+3+4⟧ And God accepts him, as one who had purposed well; … yet nevertheless a fitting injunction, saying … that the seed from him must bring to completion the good … undertaking, adding the cause: that he had poured out much blood upon the earth. ⟦q27.6|2+3+4⟧ And he obeys God who counsels him, and embraces the counsel with pleasure, and judges it more honourable than his own [?]. ⟦q27.6|2+3+4⟧ And he himself does not build the temple of God. ⟦q27.6|5+6⟧ He sends on [?] to his son abundant …, gives both the material and the pattern; being taught …, as I think, the [0637] well-built bonds of salvation. ⟦q27.6|7⟧ And, lest I lengthen my discourse going through the contemplation of each of the things said: the peaceful … able … the temple of God … these are, and if anyone …, but able to persuade to serve as bodyguard not by fear, and working together toward the building of the most divine temple, receiving harm; and Hiram king of Tyre, and the hundred and fifty thousand of the foreigners, the stone-cutters and water-carriers. ⟦q27.6|8+9⟧ But stretch forth, and prosper, and reign — even if thou hast reigned without thy Maximus — because of truth and meekness and righteousness, which are these. [n: Psal. XLIV, 5.] And I will add: and watch with all thy might not to be led away in any way with the daughters of the heathen kings, lest they join in dividing the kingdom between a foolish son and an apostate servant. ⟦q27.6|10⟧ For much willingly …. ⟦q27.6|11⟧ Farewell, honoured Father, and pray for thy servant …, grievously held by many sins.

⟦q27.7|1⟧ LETTER 43

⟦q27.7|1⟧ *Of the same, to John the Cubicularius.*

⟦q27.7|2⟧ The honoured letter of my God-guarded master came, with peace, bearing himself whole, appearing in his manners. ⟦q27.7|3⟧ For with the dignity of the words the disposition of the soul too is somehow wont to be reflected, together with the gentleness of the manners. ⟦q27.7|3⟧ And I rejoiced as though I had received my master himself, and I embraced him in the spirit, and, enfolding him whole in the room of my heart, I laid him up there; and I glorified Christ our God, who implanted in men a light, the law of love, according to which those who know how duly to tend the seeds of love can never be absent from one another, even though they stand far apart from one another in body by the distance of place.

⟦q27.7|4⟧ But since the letter, bringing good tidings of worldly peace, changed the soul's grief into joy, it is fitting that we too, for this peace, should with good reason become in every way grateful to God who granted it, lest we appear ungrateful for the kindness, repaying the benefactor ill. ⟦q27.7|5⟧ And we shall become in every way grateful by using the peace, that is to say, as is fitting, and by setting aside the friendship with the world and world-ruler which ill exists in us; and, having dissolved, even if late, the war set up against God through the passions, and having made for ourselves treaties of peace with him in the abolishing of the body, let us cease from our enmity toward him. ⟦q27.7|6⟧ For it is impossible for us to be made friends with him while we rebel against him through the passions, and consent through vice to pay tribute to the wicked tyrant and murderer of souls, the devil, unless we have first become wholly at war with the wicked one. ⟦q27.7|7⟧ For so long do we stand as enemies and foes of God, even though we [0640] mould about ourselves the name of believers, as we will to be slaves to the passions of dishonour, [n: Rom. I, 26.] and consent willingly to pay grievous tributes to the devil through the works of shame. ⟦q27.7|8⟧ And no profit will any longer come to us from the peace of the world, while the soul is ill disposed, and rebels against its own Maker, and does not consent to come under his kingship, but is still sold to ten thousand masters, who ever press it on into vice and contrive that it deceitfully choose the road that leads to destruction rather than the one that saves. ⟦q27.7|9⟧ But when we sober up from the drunkenness of the passions, and rise up against the wickedness and tyrannical kingship of the devil, and shake off from ourselves his most bitter yoke, and see how greatly the friendship with him has corrupted us, then we shall receive the peaceful coming of Christ, the peaceful and meek King, taking place invisibly in us; and we shall consent to come under his yoke, as he orders and composes us by laws of truth; and we shall accept willingly to contract friendship with him, bringing him willingly tributes, taxes: the fruits of righteousness brought to perfection in us through the virtues according to the commandment. ⟦q27.7|10⟧ Delighting in these for the sake of our salvation, he who alone is without need will, as one who does not lie, grant us all that he has promised us. ⟦q27.7|11⟧ And these are what eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it gone up into the heart of man, what God has prepared for those who love him. ⟦q27.7|12⟧ For to this end he also made us: that we might become partakers of the divine nature and sharers in its eternity, and might appear like him according to the deification that is from grace — for whose sake is the whole constitution and continuance of the things that are, and the bringing forth and coming-to-be of the things that are not.

⟦q27.7|13⟧ Let us become worthy, then, of the peace, my blessed master, manfully stripping from ourselves the passions within us that rebel against God. ⟦q27.7|14⟧ Let us waste the body as far as we have power, through which, when it swells with vigour, the devil's entry into the soul naturally comes about. ⟦q27.7|15⟧ With divine readings let us halt the wandering mind, and lead it back again to God. ⟦q27.7|16⟧ With the tensions of self-control and vigil let us temperately restrain desire, carried in disorder by appetite toward many things. ⟦q27.7|17⟧ With prayers and supplications and almsgiving let us wisely lull anger, boiling and troubled; with the fear of God and the threat of what is to come let us stop the sense-organs, moved and operating licentiously. ⟦q27.7|18⟧ Upon all things and before all let us remember death and the fearful going-out of the soul from the body; and how the principalities and authorities and powers of darkness meet it in this air, each dragging it apart toward itself and cutting it up, in proportion to the evil kinship that came to be, relationally, toward it through the passion in between. ⟦q27.7|19⟧ Let us remember the bitter pain that comes upon the soul in Hades according to conscience, at the memory of the things it did ill through the body. ⟦q27.7|20⟧ Let us remember the [0641] last consummation of the whole world, in which all this is burned up with much and immense fire: the elements fearfully shaken with the melting of the burning;
